D.3 Why FewStepOPSD resembles ReFL D.4 The velocity penalty: KL-u against KL-v D.5 Partial differentiability of the video reward License: CC BY 4.0 arXiv:2611.08240v1 [cs.LG] 05 Nov 2026 FewStepRL: Fine-tuning Few-step Diffusion Models with Reinforcement Learning Haitao Lin 1,⋆,† , Minsi Ren 2,⋆ , Yang Li 1,† , Xingtong Ge 1 , Zheyuan Hu 1 , Peiyan Hu 2,3 , Tailin Wu 2,‡ Affiliation: 1 Tencent Hunyuan, 2 Westlake University, 3 University of Chinese Academy of Sciences ⋆ Equal contribution; † Project Leader; ‡ Corresponding author: wutailin@westlake.edu.cn Abstract. Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a standard post-training method for
diffusion models, but most RL objectives roll out the current policy at every iteration, and
with a many-step sampler these rollouts typically dominate the cost of training. Few-step
models cut this cost, yet they model an average velocity over an interval,
whereas existing RL objectives are built for an instantaneous velocity with a
many-step sampler. We adapt RL objectives to few-step models systematically in
three phases, namely a transformation, a decomposition and an adaptation. The
transformation to x x -space expresses each objective we consider through a common policy
prediction, at the clean end by default and at an explicitly marked level for
transition or flow-map objectives, so that objectives stated over densities,
velocities or rewards become comparable. The decomposition writes the gradient
of each of these objectives as the sum of a self-regression toward a reference
and a reward augmentation, so that a method reduces, at the gradient level, to a choice of anchors and
weights. The adaptation computes each anchor from a few-step model at the end
of its transport or along its tangent, with the instantaneous velocity
recovered from the network itself or from an auxiliary network. This yields
few-step versions of FlowGRPO, AWM, DiffusionNFT, DiffusionOPSD and
ReFL in one modular codebase. In our experiments, reward-gradient-based
methods are the most effective where the reward is differentiable and its
gradient affordable; otherwise DiffusionNFT-style methods generally reach
high rewards in relatively few iterations despite a sometimes higher per-iteration
cost.
Extra
inference steps show no systematic aggregate benefit in our experiments, although selected
outputs visibly improve with two to four times as many sampling steps as in
training. Project Page: fewsteprl.page Code: fewsteprl-sd35 (images), fewsteprl-wan21 (videos) Checkpoints: Hugging Face (coming soon) Contents 1 Introduction Diffusion models and their flow-matching counterparts generate data by
integrating a learned transport from noise to samples, and have become a
dominant approach to image and video synthesis ( Ho et al., 2020 ; Song et al., 2021b ; Lipman et al., 2023 ; Liu et al., 2023 ; Rombach et al., 2022 ; Esser et al., 2024 ) .
Because the learned field is accurate only locally, sampling typically takes tens of
network evaluations ( Song et al., 2021a ) . Few-step models remove most of this cost by learning the transport over long
intervals directly. Consistency models and MeanFlow, which models the average
velocity over an interval, and on-policy distilled flow maps such as DMD and
AnyFlow generate in one to four evaluations ( Song et al., 2023 ; Geng et al., 2025 ; Geng et al., 2026 ; Yin et al., 2024b ; Gu et al., 2026 ) . Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a standard post-training method for
diffusion models. Fine-tuning against reward models of human preference, text rendering or
composition measurably improves the outputs ( Black et al., 2024 ; Fan et al., 2023 ; Clark et al., 2024 ; Xu et al., 2023 ; Liu et al., 2025a ; Xue et al., 2026 ; Zheng et al., 2026a ) .
This gain, however, typically requires samples from the current policy at every
iteration and must be protected against reward hacking, in which the policy
exploits the reward model instead of improving. With a many-step sampler, these rollouts typically
account for most of the
time and memory of training, and more so for long video sequences. A few-step
model shrinks this cost, but it models an average velocity over an interval,
whereas the RL objectives above are built for an instantaneous velocity or for
the transitions of a many-step sampler. Applied directly, they leave open which
network evaluation should be differentiated, how the reference policy should be
evaluated, and which parts of the interval are trained. Prior work typically resolves these
choices for one objective at a time ( Huang et al., 2026b ; Huang et al., 2026a ; Li et al., 2026b ) ; what is, to our knowledge, missing
is a systematic way to adapt RL objectives to few-step models. Figure 1 illustrates the effect of leaving them unresolved. Substituting
the average velocity of a distilled few-step model for the instantaneous
velocity required by FlowGRPO, AWM and DiffusionNFT, i.e., applying the
original objective to the distilled model without modification, gives a lower best GenEval2 than the
adapted objective derived in this paper in each of the three cases. (a) FewStepGRPO (§ 5.3 ) vs. FlowGRPO (b) MeanFlowAWM (§ 5.4 ) vs. AWM (c) MeanFlowNFT (§ 5.5 ) vs. DiffusionNFT Figure 1 : Held-out GenEval2 against the RL iteration in the mixed-reward
setting of § 6.1 , from the shared pre-RL few-step checkpoint.
Dashed: the original objective applied to the distilled model with the
average velocity substituted for the instantaneous velocity (FlowGRPO, AWM,
DiffusionNFT). Solid: the
corresponding few-step adaptation of this paper (FewStepGRPO, MeanFlowAWM,
MeanFlowNFT; § 5 ). Each annotated gap is the adaptation minus
the baseline, at the best checkpoint of the baseline and at
iteration 400 400 ; the two coincide when the baseline is best at iteration 400 400 . This paper builds one in three phases ( Figure 2 ), namely a
transformation, a decomposition and an adaptation. The transformation maps each
objective to x x -space. Under a linear noise schedule, a velocity defines an affine map
between noise levels, so each objective we consider can be expressed through a
common policy prediction, at the clean end by default and at an explicitly
marked level for transition or flow-map objectives. In x x -space, objectives stated over densities, velocities or rewards become
directly comparable. The decomposition splits the loss gradient.
The gradient of every objective we consider is the sum of a self-regression
term, which keeps the policy close to a reference, and a reward-augmentation
term, which contains the reward, so that, at the gradient level, a method reduces to a choice of
anchors and weights. The adaptation extends the anchors to few-step models. We
show
how each anchor is computed from a few-step model, either at the end of its
transport or along the tangent of the trajectory, with the instantaneous
velocity recovered from the network itself, as in MeanFlow, or from an
auxiliary network, as in DMD. All methods share one codebase, which is represented by a single training step
with several method-specific stages, namely rollout, reward and advantage, anchors
and weights, and loss (plus auxiliary fitting and the refresh of the old
policy), where each method overrides only the hooks in which it differs
(§ 5 ). The methods are therefore compared under shared rollout,
reward and evaluation code, and a new variant is specified mainly by its
anchors and weights.
Experimentally, we compare seven implementations on image generation with an
AnyFlow-distilled SD3.5-Medium ( Stability AI, 2024 ; Gu et al., 2026 ) , under
a non-differentiable OCR reward and a differentiable mixed reward, and on video
generation with an AnyFlow-distilled Wan2.1-T2V-1.3B ( Team Wan et al., 2025 ; Gu et al., 2026 ) ; we further test whether the fine-tuned
few-step models improve when sampled with more steps. The contributions of this paper are as follows. • A general view. After the transformation to x x -space, the gradients of the RL objectives considered here share one
decoupled form, the sum of a self-regression term and a reward-augmentation
term, so that their gradients differ only in their anchors and weights. • A systematic adaptation to few-step models. Each of these anchors can be
computed from an average-velocity model at the end of the transport or along its
tangent, which yields few-step versions of FlowGRPO, AWM, DiffusionNFT,
DiffusionOPSD and ReFL within one modular codebase. • Empirical guidance with experimental analysis. Where the reward is differentiable and its
gradient affordable, reward-gradient-based methods are the most effective in our experiments, and ReFL
alone is a strong baseline. Otherwise, group-advantage methods apply. Among
them, DiffusionNFT-style methods generally reach high rewards in relatively few
iterations, even when one iteration is expensive, and DMD-based variants are
faster per iteration on image but slower on video, depending on the relative
cost of forward and backward passes, and often coincide with larger
held-out degradation. Additional inference steps bring little aggregate
improvement in generation quality, while visual gains appear in selected outputs with
more sampling steps than training. 2 Background Diffusion and flow-based generators. Diffusion models learn to reverse a fixed noising process ( Ho et al., 2020 ) ; score-based models cast this reversal as a reverse-time
SDE whose probability-flow ODE gives a deterministic sampler, of which DDIM is
a discrete instance ( Song et al., 2021b ; Song et al., 2021a ) . Flow matching and
rectified flow instead regress the velocity of a prescribed interpolation
between noise and data, the latter straightening the paths so that fewer
integration steps suffice ( Lipman et al., 2023 ; Liu et al., 2023 ) .
These models were scaled by moving to a latent space ( Rombach et al., 2022 ) , by
transformer backbones ( Peebles and Xie, 2023 ; Ma et al., 2024 ) and by larger, more
efficient architectures ( Podell et al., 2024 ; Esser et al., 2024 ; Xie et al., 2025a ) ,
with classifier-free guidance as a standard way to trade diversity for
prompt fidelity ( Ho and Salimans, 2021 ) . Related generators include
current text-to-image systems ( OpenAI, 2022 ; Betker et al., 2023 ; Saharia et al., 2022 ; Black Forest Labs, 2024 ; Black Forest Labs, 2025 ; Team Seedream et al., 2025 ; Z-Image Team et al., 2025 ; Qin et al., 2025 ) ,
multimodal models that both generate and understand images in one network ( Chen et al., 2025b ; Ma et al., 2025a ; Xie et al., 2025b ; Wang et al., 2024b ; OpenAI et al., 2024 ; Chen et al., 2025a ) ,
trained on web-scale image–text data ( Schuhmann et al., 2022 ; Zou, 2024 ) , and video
generators such as Wan and LongCat-Video ( Team Wan et al., 2025 ; Meituan LongCat Team et al., 2025 ) . These models typically learn the
instantaneous velocity of the sampling flow, or an affine transformation of it
such as the score or the predicted noise. Since this velocity is instantaneous,
the sampler integrates it with many small steps, typically 32 or 64, which
makes inference costly. Few-step generation. Sampling cost has been cut along several lines. Progressive distillation halves
the number of steps repeatedly by training a student to match two teacher
steps with one ( Salimans and Ho, 2022 ) . Consistency models map every
point of a trajectory to its endpoint, and were extended to latent space,
arbitrary trajectory jumps, multiple sub-intervals and segmented trajectories ( Song et al., 2023 ; Luo et al., 2023a ; Kim et al., 2024 ; Wang et al., 2024a ; Ren et al., 2024 ) ;
consistency has also been used as a training regulariser against sampling
drift and to learn from noisy data ( Daras et al., 2023 ; Daras et al., 2024 ) . Shortcut
models condition on the step size and learn to take large steps directly ( Frans et al., 2025 ) . Adversarial distillation adds a discriminator so
that one to four steps produce sharp samples ( Wang et al., 2023a ; Sauer et al., 2025 ; Sauer et al., 2024 ; Lin et al., 2024 ; Chadebec et al., 2025 ; Lu and Song, 2025 ) .
Distribution matching distillation (DMD) trains the generator so that its
output distribution matches the teacher’s, using the difference between a
teacher score and the score of an auxiliary network fitted to the generator’s
samples ( Yin et al., 2024b ; Yin et al., 2024a ) , an idea shared with Diff-Instruct and
variational score distillation ( Luo et al., 2023b ; Wang et al., 2023b ) ;
later work scales it to flow models, splits it into
sub-intervals, decouples guidance from matching, moves it to continuous time,
regularises it with consistency, and adapts it to video ( Ge et al., 2026a ; Fan et al., 2026b ; Liu et al., 2026a ; Liu et al., 2026b ; Zheng et al., 2026b ; Ge et al., 2026b ; Nie et al., 2026 ; Luo et al., 2025 ) .
Flow maps learn the transport between any two noise levels. MeanFlow
parameterises the average velocity over an interval and trains it through its
identity with the instantaneous velocity ( Geng et al., 2025 ; Lin et al., 2026 ; Geng et al., 2026 ) , and AnyFlow distils such a
map on policy for any step count ( Gu et al., 2026 ) . Unlike the many-step
generators above, these flow-map models learn an average velocity over an
interval, which can be read as the direction of a secant of the sampling
trajectory, differing substantially from the instantaneous velocity where the
trajectory is curved. Reinforcement learning for diffusion models. RL fine-tuning of generators builds on policy optimisation ( Levine, 2018 ; Schulman et al., 2017 ) , preference optimisation ( Rafailov et al., 2023 ) and group-relative advantages, which normalise rewards
within a group of samples for the same prompt ( Shao et al., 2024 ; Guo et al., 2025 ) . For diffusion models, early
work fine-tunes on human feedback ( Lee et al., 2023 ) , treats denoising as
a Markov decision process optimised by policy gradients ( Black et al., 2024 ; Fan et al., 2023 ) , or optimises pairwise preferences directly ( Wallace et al., 2024 ) . FlowGRPO brings group-relative policy
optimisation to flow models by turning the ODE sampler into an SDE with
tractable transition likelihoods ( Liu et al., 2025a ) ; follow-ups extend it
to video, mix ODE and SDE steps, weight timesteps, and preserve the noise
coefficients of the sampler ( Xue et al., 2025 ; Li et al., 2026a ; He et al., 2026 ; Wang and Yu, 2025 ) . A
second family avoids transition likelihoods by acting on the forward process:
AWM weights the pretraining loss by the advantage ( Xue et al., 2026 ) ,
reward-weighted and policy-gradient flow matching reweight or rescale the
velocity regression ( Fan et al., 2025 ; McAllister et al., 2026 ) ,
adjoint matching is scaled to RL post-training ( Bergmeister et al., 2026 ) , and
DiffusionNFT contrasts positive and negative implicit policies built from the
model and the frozen policy that generated its rollouts ( Zheng et al., 2026a ) , following NFT in
language models ( Chen et al., 2026b ) . A third family backpropagates
differentiable rewards through the sampler, as in DRaFT and ReFL ( Clark et al., 2024 ; Xu et al., 2023 ; Wang et al., 2026 ) ; the recently
proposed DiffusionOPSD ( Zhou et al., 2026 ) belongs to this family
but constructs detached points with normalised reward-gradient steps and fits
the policy to them, thereby forming a surrogate gradient. These methods are
built for an instantaneous velocity, and most of them need on-policy
rollouts at every iteration, which are expensive with the many-step samplers discussed above; how
to apply RL to few-step models is therefore an important question. RL for few-step generators. Closest to this paper are methods that fine-tune few-step models with rewards.
Several combine DMD with RL, by adding a reward term to distillation, guiding
distillation with reward gradients, or recasting distribution matching itself
as a reward ( Chen et al., 2026a ; Jiang et al., 2026 ; Dong et al., 2026 ; Fan et al., 2026a ) ;
others tune distilled models with non-differentiable rewards,
Kullback–Leibler (KL) regularised
alignment or pairwise sample optimisation ( Luo et al., 2026 ; Luo, 2025 ; Miao et al., 2025 ) . For flow maps,
Flow-Map GRPO composes stochastic steps of a flow-map generator ( Li et al., 2026b ) , MeanFlowNFT applies DiffusionNFT to an
average-velocity model through its self-induced instantaneous field ( Huang et al., 2026b ) , and RTDMD optimises a reward-tilted distribution
matching objective ( Huang et al., 2026a ) . These works mostly adapt one objective
to one kind of few-step model; we instead give a common construction that
extends FlowGRPO, AWM, DiffusionNFT, DiffusionOPSD and ReFL to
average-velocity models, and compare them under one implementation. 3 Preliminaries 3.1 Few-step Diffusion Models with On-Policy Distillation We write t ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] t\in[0,1] for the noise level, with data at t = 0 t=0 and Gaussian

DMD. Distribution matching distillation ( Yin et al., 2024b ; Yin et al., 2024a ) does not regress u θ u_{\theta} onto a target. It
maintains a second, auxiliary velocity v ϕ ​ ( x ~ t , t ) v_{\phi}(\tilde{x}_{t},t) with its own
parameters ϕ \phi , fitted online by flow matching to the samples generated by
the student. Its target is the conditional velocity of each sample instead of
the teacher, because v ϕ v_{\phi} is meant to approximate the velocity of p θ p_{\theta} instead of p data p_{\mathrm{data}} : ℒ aux = 𝔼 x ^ ∼ p θ , ϵ , t ​ [ ‖ v ϕ ​ ( x ~ t , t ) − v cond ‖ 2 ] , x ~ t = AddNoise ​ ( x ^ , t ) , v cond = ϵ − x ^ . \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{aux}}=\mathbb{E}_{\,\hat{x}\sim p_{\theta},\ \epsilon,\ t}\Big[\left\lVert v_{\phi}(\tilde{x}_{t},t)-v_{\mathrm{cond}}\right\rVert^{2}\Big],\qquad\tilde{x}_{t}=\textsc{AddNoise}(\hat{x},t),\quad v_{\mathrm{cond}}=\epsilon-\hat{x}. train u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , r ) u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r) : ∇ θ ℒ DMD = 𝔼 ϵ , t ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) ​ [ 1 − t t ​ sg ⁡ [ v real ​ ( x ~ t , t ) − v ϕ ​ ( x ~ t , t ) ] ⊤ ​ ∂ x ^ θ ∂ θ ] , \nabla_{\theta}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{DMD}}=\mathbb{E}_{\,\epsilon,\,t\in(0,1)}\Big[\frac{1-t}{t}\,\operatorname{sg}\big[v_{\mathrm{real}}(\tilde{x}_{t},t)-v_{\phi}(\tilde{x}_{t},t)\big]^{\!\top}\frac{\partial\,\hat{x}_{\theta}}{\partial\theta}\Big], (10) The order of the two fields is fixed by the conversion between them. The
score of the linear path is ∇ log p t ( x ~ t ) = − 𝔼 [ ϵ ∣ x ~ t ] / t \nabla\log p_{t}(\tilde{x}_{t})=-\mathbb{E}[\epsilon\mid\tilde{x}_{t}]/t with 𝔼 ⁡ [ ϵ ∣ x ~ t ] = x ~ t + ( 1 − t ) ​ v \mathbb{E}[\epsilon\mid\tilde{x}_{t}]=\tilde{x}_{t}+(1-t)v , so the difference ∇ log ⁡ p t ϕ − ∇ log ⁡ p t real \nabla\log p^{\phi}_{t}-\nabla\log p^{\mathrm{real}}_{t} of the scores of the two
fields, which the reverse KL produces, equals 1 − t t ​ ( v real − v ϕ ) \frac{1-t}{t}\,(v_{\mathrm{real}}-v_{\phi}) . Converting scores to velocities reverses the
order of the two fields and introduces the score-to-velocity factor 1 − t t \frac{1-t}{t} , which is positive on ( 0 , 1 ) (0,1) and is absorbed into a per- t t weight in practice. Descending Equation ( 10 ) therefore applies the
direction + ( v ϕ − v real ) +(v_{\phi}-v_{\mathrm{real}}) , reducing the mismatch to the data field
relative to the auxiliary field that tracks p θ p_{\theta} . The term ∂ x ^ θ / ∂ θ \partial\hat{x}_{\theta}/\partial\theta requires a differentiable sampler, and
the sampler used in practice is stochastic. A step at level t t does not move
directly to the next level; it first maps the state to r = 0 r=0 with one network
evaluation and then renoises the result to an intermediate level t ′ t^{\prime} with 0 < t ′ < t 0<t^{\prime}<t : x ^ θ , 0 = x ^ θ , t − t ​ u θ ​ ( x ^ θ , t , t , 0 ) , x ^ θ , t ′ = ( 1 − t ′ ) ​ x ^ θ , 0 + t ′ ​ ϵ ′ , ϵ ′ ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( 0 , I ) . \hat{x}_{\theta,0}=\hat{x}_{\theta,t}-t\,u_{\theta}\big(\hat{x}_{\theta,t},t,0\big),\qquad\hat{x}_{\theta,t^{\prime}}=(1-t^{\prime})\,\hat{x}_{\theta,0}+t^{\prime}\,\epsilon^{\prime},\qquad\epsilon^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I). (11) Unlike the deterministic chain Equation ( 5 ), this sampler draws fresh
noise ϵ ′ \epsilon^{\prime} at every step, so the state at level t ′ t^{\prime} is not a
deterministic function of the previous state. The levels follow a fixed grid of M step + 1 M_{\mathrm{step}}+1 values spanning M step M_{\mathrm{step}} intervals; a step count ℓ ∈ { 1 , … , M step } \ell\in\{1,\dots,M_{\mathrm{step}}\} is drawn
per update, and only the last step remains in the computation graph,
with all earlier steps detached. The backward pass therefore requires one
network evaluation for any ℓ \ell . Moreover, the differentiated evaluation is u θ ​ ( ⋅ , t , 0 ) u_{\theta}(\cdot,t,0) at the level that the grid assigns to step ℓ \ell , so
randomising ℓ \ell changes this level across updates. Over training, the
gradient therefore reaches u θ ​ ( ⋅ , t , 0 ) u_{\theta}(\cdot,t,0) at every grid level t t instead
of a single one. 3.2 Reinforcement Learning for Diffusion Models FlowGRPO. FlowGRPO combines a GRPO-style group baseline with a PPO transition objective ( Schulman et al., 2017 ; Shao et al., 2024 ; Liu et al., 2025a ) . It scores a group of K K rollouts that share a
prompt c c , and turns the raw scores into a group-normalised advantage. Writing R ( i ) = R ⁡ ( 𝒟 ⁡ ( x ^ ( i ) ) , c ) R^{(i)}=R\big(\mathcal{D}(\hat{x}^{(i)}),c\big) for the endpoint reward of member i i ( 𝒟 \mathcal{D} the decoder), the advantage standardises the reward within the group, A ( i ) = R ( i ) − mean ⁡ ( { R } ) std ⁡ ( { R } ) + ε std , i = 1 , … , K . A^{(i)}=\frac{R^{(i)}-\operatorname{mean}\big(\{R\}\big)}{\operatorname{std}\big(\{R\}\big)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{std}}},\qquad i=1,\dots,K. (12) Here { R } \{R\} denotes the K K rewards of the group and ε std > 0 \varepsilon_{\mathrm{std}}>0 is a small
floor. The advantage is a detached per-sample scalar with zero mean across the
group; no value
network is fitted, and the group mean serves as the baseline. AWM below uses
the same A ( i ) A^{(i)} ; DiffusionNFT uses a bounded transform of it. FlowGRPO formulates the stochastic differential equation (SDE) sampler as a
Markov decision process, whose state is the current noisy sample x t x_{t} (a
point on the sampling trajectory, in the convention of
§ 3 ) together
with the prompt and the level and whose action is the next state produced by
the sampler; the Gaussian transition p θ tr ​ ( x r ∣ x t ) p^{\mathrm{tr}}_{\theta}(x_{r}\mid x_{t}) then
provides a likelihood, which the deterministic sampler lacks. Optimisation is
proximal policy optimisation on the realised action x r x_{r} , ℒ GRPO = − 𝔼 i , t ​ [ min ⁡ { A ( i ) ​ ρ tr ( i ) , A ( i ) ​ clip ⁡ ( ρ tr ( i ) , 1 ± ε clip ) } ] , ρ tr = p θ tr ​ ( x r ∣ x t ) p old tr ​ ( x r ∣ x t ) . \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GRPO}}=-\,\mathbb{E}_{\,i,\,t}\Big[\min\big\{A^{(i)}\rho_{\mathrm{tr}}^{(i)},\;A^{(i)}\operatorname{clip}\big(\rho_{\mathrm{tr}}^{(i)},1\pm\varepsilon_{\mathrm{clip}}\big)\big\}\Big],\qquad\rho_{\mathrm{tr}}=\frac{p^{\mathrm{tr}}_{\theta}(x_{r}\mid x_{t})}{p^{\mathrm{tr}}_{\mathrm{old}}(x_{r}\mid x_{t})}. (13) Here the old policy is the frozen policy, with parameters θ old \theta_{\mathrm{old}} , that generated the rollouts on which the current policy
is trained; it receives no gradient. In our implementation, θ old \theta_{\mathrm{old}} is an exponential moving average (EMA) of θ \theta ,
refreshed after every outer iteration (§ 5.1 ) with the
EMA-decay ratio as the weight kept on the previous θ old \theta_{\mathrm{old}} ; an
EMA-decay ratio of 0.0 0.0 therefore copies θ \theta , so that θ old = θ \theta_{\mathrm{old}}=\theta at the start of every outer iteration. The
subscript old \mathrm{old} marks its quantities, so p old tr p^{\mathrm{tr}}_{\mathrm{old}} is its transition density, v old v_{\mathrm{old}} its instantaneous velocity and p old p_{\mathrm{old}} the distribution of its
rollouts. The clipping radius is ε clip \varepsilon_{\mathrm{clip}} . For later
clipped surrogates, define the detached unclipped-gradient gate χ ⁡ ( z , A ) := [ ( A > 0 ∧ z ≤ 1 + ε clip ) ∨ ( A < 0 ∧ z ≥ 1 − ε clip ) ] , χ ⁡ ( z , 0 ) := 0 . \chi(z,A):=\mathbf{1}\!\left[(A>0\ \wedge\ z\leq 1+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{clip}})\ \vee\ (A<0\ \wedge\ z\geq 1-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{clip}})\right],\qquad\chi(z,0):=0. (14) At the two clipping kinks this convention selects the derivative of the
unclipped branch.
The sign of the reward advantage determines the direction of the update of the
mean μ θ \mu_{\theta} of the Gaussian transition (the scoring kernel defined in Equation ( 95 )) relative to the visited noisy state x r x_{r} . AWM. Advantage Weighted Matching ( Xue et al., 2026 ) keeps the
signed advantage but does not evaluate a likelihood on the reverse process.
Each update draws a fresh ( t , ϵ ) (t,\epsilon) and constructs both the noised state
and the regression target from the rollout sample, x ~ t ( i ) = AddNoise ​ ( x ^ ( i ) , t ) = ( 1 − t ) ​ x ^ ( i ) + t ​ ϵ , v cond ( i ) = ϵ − x ^ ( i ) , \tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)}=\textsc{AddNoise}\big(\hat{x}^{(i)},t\big)=(1-t)\,\hat{x}^{(i)}+t\,\epsilon,\qquad v_{\mathrm{cond}}^{(i)}=\epsilon-\hat{x}^{(i)}, (15) where v cond ( i ) v_{\mathrm{cond}}^{(i)} is the conditional velocity , i.e., the velocity of
the straight path from x ^ ( i ) \hat{x}^{(i)} to ϵ \epsilon , which is computed from the two
endpoints without a network evaluation. AWM then observes that the
flow-matching loss against that target is, for a suitable time weight w ⁡ ( t ) w(t) , an evidence-lower-bound
surrogate for the sequence-level log-likelihood of the clean sample, log ⁡ p θ ​ ( x ^ ( i ) ∣ c ) ≥ log ⁡ p ^ θ ​ ( x ^ ( i ) ∣ c ) + const , log ⁡ p ^ θ ​ ( x ^ ( i ) ∣ c ) := − 𝔼 t , ϵ ​ [ w ⁡ ( t ) ​ ‖ v θ ​ ( x ~ t ( i ) , t ) − v cond ( i ) ‖ 2 ] , \log p_{\theta}\big(\hat{x}^{(i)}\mid c\big)\;\geq\;\log\hat{p}_{\theta}\big(\hat{x}^{(i)}\mid c\big)+\mathrm{const},\qquad\log\hat{p}_{\theta}\big(\hat{x}^{(i)}\mid c\big):=-\,\mathbb{E}_{\,t,\,\epsilon}\Big[w(t)\,\left\lVert v_{\theta}\big(\tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)},t\big)-v_{\mathrm{cond}}^{(i)}\right\rVert^{2}\Big], (16) so that the policy-gradient term A ( i ) ​ ∇ θ ​ log ⁡ p ^ θ A^{(i)}\nabla_{\theta}\log\hat{p}_{\theta} reduces to the pretraining objective reweighted per sample: ℒ AWM = 𝔼 i , t , ϵ ​ [ A ( i ) ​ w ​ ( t ) ​ ‖ v θ ​ ( x ~ t ( i ) , t ) − v cond ( i ) ‖ 2 ] , \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{AWM}}=\mathbb{E}_{\,i,\,t,\,\epsilon}\Big[A^{(i)}\,w(t)\,\left\lVert v_{\theta}\big(\tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)},t\big)-v_{\mathrm{cond}}^{(i)}\right\rVert^{2}\Big], (17) with the time weight w ⁡ ( t ) = 1 w(t)=1 in the original AWM (our implementation
effectively uses w ⁡ ( t ) = τ AWM 2 w(t)=\tau_{\mathrm{AWM}}^{2} , § 5.4 ) and
a velocity-space proximal penalty ‖ v θ − v old ‖ 2 \left\lVert v_{\theta}-v_{\mathrm{old}}\right\rVert^{2} against the old policy v old v_{\mathrm{old}} , called a KL term in AWM, added for stability. Minimising Equation ( 17 ) decreases the matching residual at x ^ ( i ) \hat{x}^{(i)} when A ( i ) > 0 A^{(i)}>0 and increases it when A ( i ) < 0 A^{(i)}<0 . Since no reverse-time
discretisation appears, the sampler can be chosen freely. DiffusionNFT. Negative-aware fine-tuning ( Zheng et al., 2026a ) builds on an optimality-variable construction ( Levine, 2018 ) and requires neither a likelihood nor a signed
weight. It first maps the advantage to a bounded optimality probability with a normaliser Z c = std ⁡ ( { R } ) + ε std Z_{c}=\operatorname{std}(\{R\})+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{std}} , r ^ ( i ) = 1 2 + 1 2 ​ clip ⁡ [ R ( i ) − mean ⁡ ( { R } ) Z c , − 1 , 1 ] = 1 2 + 1 2 ​ clip ⁡ ( A ( i ) , − 1 , 1 ) ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] , \hat{r}^{(i)}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\,\operatorname{clip}\!\left[\frac{R^{(i)}-\operatorname{mean}\big(\{R\}\big)}{Z_{c}},\;-1,\;1\right]=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\,\operatorname{clip}\big(A^{(i)},\,-1,\,1\big)\;\in[0,1], (18) since the bracketed quotient then equals Equation ( 12 ). Thus A ( i ) A^{(i)} is signed,
unclipped and zero-mean under Equation ( 12 ) and enters as a multiplier , whereas r ^ ( i ) \hat{r}^{(i)} is bounded, monotone in A ( i ) A^{(i)} and equal to 1 2 \tfrac{1}{2} when A ( i ) = 0 A^{(i)}=0 , and enters as a mixing weight . A positive advantage gives r ^ ( i ) > 1 2 \hat{r}^{(i)}>\tfrac{1}{2} and
hence a larger weight on the positive branch. Each update then draws a fresh ( t , ϵ ) (t,\epsilon) and forms the noised state x ~ t ( i ) \tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)} and the conditional velocity v cond ( i ) v_{\mathrm{cond}}^{(i)} as in Equation ( 15 ); the target is the same as in AWM, and, besides the weight, only the regressed
quantity differs. Two implicit policies are built from one
trainable network v θ v_{\theta} and the frozen old policy v old v_{\mathrm{old}} , both evaluated at that same x ~ t ( i ) \tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)} and mixed by a
branch coefficient β \beta : v θ ± ​ ( x ~ t ( i ) , t ) := ( 1 ∓ β ) ​ v old ​ ( x ~ t ( i ) , t ) ± β ​ v θ ​ ( x ~ t ( i ) , t ) . v^{\pm}_{\theta}\big(\tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)},t\big):=(1\mp\beta)\,v_{\mathrm{old}}\big(\tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)},t\big)\pm\beta\,v_{\theta}\big(\tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)},t\big). (19) Both are regressed onto the same target v cond ( i ) v_{\mathrm{cond}}^{(i)} , mixed by r ^ ( i ) \hat{r}^{(i)} : ℒ NFT = 𝔼 i , t , ϵ ​ [ r ^ ( i ) ​ ‖ v θ + − v cond ( i ) ‖ 2 + ( 1 − r ^ ( i ) ) ​ ‖ v θ − − v cond ( i ) ‖ 2 ] . \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{NFT}}=\mathbb{E}_{\,i,\,t,\,\epsilon}\Big[\hat{r}^{(i)}\,\left\lVert v^{+}_{\theta}-v_{\mathrm{cond}}^{(i)}\right\rVert^{2}+\big(1-\hat{r}^{(i)}\big)\,\left\lVert v^{-}_{\theta}-v_{\mathrm{cond}}^{(i)}\right\rVert^{2}\Big]. (20) Both branches are affine in the same trainable network v θ v_{\theta} ; since v old v_{\mathrm{old}} , v cond ( i ) v_{\mathrm{cond}}^{(i)} and r ^ ( i ) \hat{r}^{(i)} are detached, v θ v_{\theta} is the only differentiated quantity in Equation ( 20 ). 4 Framework Figure 2 : Overview of the framework. (a) RL increases the relative mass of the
high-reward mode under the rollout distribution p old p_{\mathrm{old}} of the
old policy. The default
objective is evaluated at the clean end, on the policy prediction
(renoise-and-denoise sample) x ~ θ = X ⁡ [ x ~ t , v θ , 0 ] = x ~ t − t ​ v θ \tilde{x}_{\theta}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,v_{\theta},\,0\big]=\tilde{x}_{t}-t\,v_{\theta} ,
whose reward augmentation attracts it toward high-reward rollout samples and
repels it from low-reward ones; in the figure, x ^ + \hat{x}^{+} and x ^ − \hat{x}^{-} denote a high-reward and a low-reward rollout sample x ^ ( i ) \hat{x}^{(i)} ,
respectively. (b) The RL-loss gradient can be decoupled into a
self-regression term, visualised as a regression force toward the regression
anchor x ~ reg \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}} , and a reward-augmentation term, visualised as a reward force
along the displacement x ~ rwd − x ~ base \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd}}-\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}} from the baseline anchor x ~ base \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}} , which
lies between the two modes, to the rewarded anchor x ~ rwd \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd}} . A high-reward
sample produces attraction along that displacement (blue); a low-reward
sample produces repulsion against it (orange), so both updates favour higher reward
(§ 4.2 ). (c) For a few-step model, the prediction and the anchors { x ~ rwd , x ~ base , x ~ reg } \{\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd}},\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}},\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}}\} are computed either at the end
of the transport curve, x ~ t − t ​ u \tilde{x}_{t}-t\,u (u-form), or along the tangent
at x ~ t \tilde{x}_{t} , x ~ t − t ​ v \tilde{x}_{t}-t\,v (v-form). The instantaneous velocity is recovered from
the network itself for
MeanFlow, v = u + ( t − r ) ​ sg ⁡ ( ∂ t u ) v=u+(t-r)\,\operatorname{sg}(\partial_{t}u) with the detached correction
evaluated on the old policy in the implemented methods ( Equation ( 56 )),
and from an auxiliary network for DMD, v = v ϕ v=v_{\phi} , fitted so that v ϕ ​ ( x ~ t , t ) ≈ 𝔼 ⁡ [ ϵ − x ^ ∣ x ~ t ] v_{\phi}(\tilde{x}_{t},t)\approx\mathbb{E}\big[\epsilon-\hat{x}\mid\tilde{x}_{t}\big] (§ 4.3 ). Each RL method of § 3.2 increases the relative probability of
high-reward samples under the rollout distribution p old p_{\mathrm{old}} of the
old policy. The methods
differ in the space in which the update is formulated (transition densities,
velocities, or rewards differentiated through the sample) and in how a
few-step network computes the quantities that the update requires. This
section resolves both differences in three steps, summarised in Figure 2 . (i) Transformation to x x -space (§ 4.1 , Figure 2 a). Under the linear noise schedule a velocity transports a state
affinely, so each objective of § 3 can be expressed through a
common prediction in x x -space. By default this prediction is the policy
prediction x ~ θ \tilde{x}_{\theta} ; MeanFlow and FewStepGRPO keep an explicitly marked level- r r quantity.
In x x -space the methods become directly comparable. (ii) Decomposition of the gradient (§ 4.2 , Figure 2 b). In x x -space, the gradient of each of these losses is the transposed Jacobian ( ∂ x ~ θ / ∂ θ ) ⊤ (\partial\tilde{x}_{\theta}/\partial\theta)^{\top} applied to the sum of a reward-free
self-regression toward an anchor x ~ reg \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}} , which limits how far the prediction
moves, and a reward augmentation along x ~ rwd − x ~ base \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd}}-\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}} , which contains the
reward. A method is then specified by the prediction, three anchors ( x ~ reg , x ~ rwd , x ~ base ) (\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}},\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd}},\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}) and three weights ( W reg , W rwd , ω ) (W_{\mathrm{reg}},W_{\mathrm{rwd}},\omega) instead of a new loss. (iii) Adaptation to few-step models (§ 4.3 , Figure 2 c). The framework requires instantaneous velocities,
whereas a few-step model outputs an average velocity. Its anchors are
therefore computed either at the end of the transport curve (u-form) or along
the tangent at the current state (v-form). The latter recovers the
instantaneous velocity from the network itself, as in MeanFlow, or from an
auxiliary network, as in DMD. § 5 then specifies the predictions, anchors and weights of each
family. 4.1 Transformation to x-space The objectives of § 3 are written in transition-density or
velocity space. Under the linear noise schedule, a velocity instead identifies a
point in x x -space at any chosen level r r . This x x -space includes noisy
level- r r points; clean samples correspond to r = 0 r=0 . For any point x t x_{t} at level t t (in particular a renoised state x ~ t \tilde{x}_{t} ),
define the affine transport X ⁡ [ x t , v , r ] := x t − ( t − r ) ​ v . X\big[x_{t},\,v,\,r\big]\;:=\;x_{t}-(t-r)\,v. (21) For two velocities evaluated at the same x t x_{t} and transported to the same
level r < t r<t , ‖ v a − v b ‖ 2 = 1 ( t − r ) 2 ​ ‖ X ⁡ [ x t , v a , r ] − X ⁡ [ x t , v b , r ] ‖ 2 . \left\lVert v_{a}-v_{b}\right\rVert^{2}\;=\;\frac{1}{(t-r)^{2}}\,\left\lVert X\big[x_{t},\,v_{a},\,r\big]-X\big[x_{t},\,v_{b},\,r\big]\right\rVert^{2}. (22) Thus a velocity MSE equals a scaled MSE between transported points in x x -space. At the clean level r = 0 r=0 the factor is t − 2 t^{-2} , and we omit the
level subscript.
In particular, for x ~ t = ( 1 − t ) ​ x + t ​ ϵ \tilde{x}_{t}=(1-t)x+t\epsilon and v cond = ϵ − x v_{\mathrm{cond}}=\epsilon-x , Equation ( 21 ) gives X ⁡ [ x ~ t , v cond , 0 ] = x X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,v_{\mathrm{cond}},\,0\big]=x . For the reward objectives below, a detached rollout endpoint x ^ ( i ) \hat{x}^{(i)} is
re-noised to x ~ t ( i ) \tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)} , and the policy provides the current policy prediction x ~ θ ( i ) = X ⁡ [ x ~ t ( i ) , v θ , 0 ] = x ~ t ( i ) − t ​ v θ , \tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)}\;=\;X\big[\tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)},\,v_{\theta},\,0\big]\;=\;\tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)}-t\,v_{\theta}, (23) while the conditional target maps back to x ^ ( i ) \hat{x}^{(i)} . Hence the loss compares
predictions at the same state x ~ t ( i ) \tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)} , and only x ~ θ ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)} is
differentiated. § 4.3 constructs this prediction when the network
outputs an average velocity instead of an instantaneous one.
Analysing a loss in x x -space therefore shows how the prediction x ~ θ \tilde{x}_{\theta} must
change to reduce the loss, i.e., which points x ~ θ \tilde{x}_{\theta} moves toward and which it
moves away from. The same map applied to the old policy gives the old policy’s prediction x ~ old ( i ) = X ⁡ [ x ~ t ( i ) , v old , 0 ] , \tilde{x}^{(i)}_{\mathrm{old}}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)},\,v_{\mathrm{old}},\,0\big], (24) which serves as an anchor in several methods below; its few-step variants,
the self-induced prediction of Example 5 and the u-prediction and J J -free query of § 4.3 , are collected in Appendix A . Example 1 (AWM in x-space) . The target of Equation ( 17 ) is v cond ( i ) v_{\mathrm{cond}}^{(i)} , and Equation ( 21 ) sends it to the rollout endpoint
itself, X ⁡ [ x ~ t ( i ) , v cond ( i ) , 0 ] = x ^ ( i ) X\big[\tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)},\,v_{\mathrm{cond}}^{(i)},\,0\big]=\hat{x}^{(i)} . Applying Equation ( 22 ), ℒ AWM = 𝔼 i , t , ϵ ​ [ A ( i ) ​ w ⁡ ( t ) t 2 ​ ‖ x ~ θ ( i ) − x ^ ( i ) ‖ 2 ] . \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{AWM}}=\mathbb{E}_{\,i,\,t,\,\epsilon}\Big[A^{(i)}\,\frac{w(t)}{t^{2}}\;\left\lVert\tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)}-\hat{x}^{(i)}\right\rVert^{2}\Big]. (25) The method is a squared residual between the current prediction and the
model’s sample, weighted by the signed advantage. Taking w ⁡ ( t ) = t 2 w(t)=t^{2} cancels the prefactor and gives an x x -space weight independent of t t ;
this is the choice our few-step implementation makes up to a floor
(§ 5.4 ). Example 2 (DiffusionNFT in x-space) . The target of Equation ( 20 ) is again v cond ( i ) v_{\mathrm{cond}}^{(i)} , so Equation ( 21 )
sends it to the same rollout endpoint x ^ ( i ) \hat{x}^{(i)} . Only the prediction
differs; the two implicit policies Equation ( 19 ) are mapped to x ~ θ ± = X ⁡ [ x ~ t ( i ) , v θ ± , 0 ] = x ~ old ( i ) ± β ⁡ ( x ~ θ ( i ) − x ~ old ( i ) ) , \tilde{x}^{\pm}_{\theta}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)},\,v^{\pm}_{\theta},\,0\big]=\tilde{x}^{(i)}_{\mathrm{old}}\pm\beta\big(\tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)}-\tilde{x}^{(i)}_{\mathrm{old}}\big), (26) with x ~ old ( i ) \tilde{x}^{(i)}_{\mathrm{old}} as in Equation ( 24 ).
Applying Equation ( 22 ) to each branch, ℒ NFT = 𝔼 i , t , ϵ ​ [ 1 t 2 ​ ( r ^ ( i ) ​ ‖ x ~ θ + − x ^ ( i ) ‖ 2 + ( 1 − r ^ ( i ) ) ​ ‖ x ~ θ − − x ^ ( i ) ‖ 2 ) ] . \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{NFT}}=\mathbb{E}_{\,i,\,t,\,\epsilon}\Big[\frac{1}{t^{2}}\Big(\hat{r}^{(i)}\,\left\lVert\tilde{x}^{+}_{\theta}-\hat{x}^{(i)}\right\rVert^{2}+\big(1-\hat{r}^{(i)}\big)\,\left\lVert\tilde{x}^{-}_{\theta}-\hat{x}^{(i)}\right\rVert^{2}\Big)\Big]. (27) The method mixes two squared residuals to the model’s sample with weights r ^ ( i ) \hat{r}^{(i)} and 1 − r ^ ( i ) 1-\hat{r}^{(i)} . The positive branch receives more weight
when the reward of the sample is above the group mean, and the negative branch
when it is below. The common prefactor t − 2 t^{-2} can be absorbed into the
weights, so the mixing itself is independent of t t . 4.2 The Decoupled Loss Decomposition Under the transformation Equation ( 21 ), the gradient of each objective
considered here is the transposed Jacobian ( ∂ x ~ θ ( i ) / ∂ θ ) ⊤ (\partial\tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)}/\partial\theta)^{\top} applied to the sum of two vectors
in x x -space (at the clean level by default and at level r r in the explicit
exceptions). We adopt this form as the general framework, ∇ θ ℒ ( i ) = 2 ​ ( ∂ x ~ θ ( i ) ∂ θ ) ⊤ ​ [ W reg ( i ) ​ ( x ~ θ ( i ) − x ~ reg ( i ) ) ⏟ self-regression − W rwd ( i ) ​ ω ( i ) ​ ( x ~ rwd ( i ) − x ~ base ( i ) ) ⏟ reward augmentation ] , \nabla_{\theta}\mathcal{L}^{(i)}=2\left(\frac{\partial\tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)}}{\partial\theta}\right)^{\!\!\top}\Big[\;\underbrace{W_{\mathrm{reg}}^{(i)}\big(\tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)}-\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}}^{(i)}\big)}_{\text{self-regression}}\;-\;\underbrace{W_{\mathrm{rwd}}^{(i)}\;\omega^{(i)}\big(\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd}}^{(i)}-\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}^{(i)}\big)}_{\text{reward augmentation}}\Big], (28) with W reg ( i ) , W rwd ( i ) ≥ 0 W_{\mathrm{reg}}^{(i)},W_{\mathrm{rwd}}^{(i)}\geq 0 and ω ( i ) ∈ ℝ \omega^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R} detached scalars. The factor 2 2 from differentiating a
square is kept outside the bracket, so all weights below are defined relative
to Equation ( 28 ); for example, the loss W ​ ‖ x ~ θ − x ~ rwd ‖ 2 W\left\lVert\tilde{x}_{\theta}-\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd}}\right\rVert^{2} corresponds to W rwd = W W_{\mathrm{rwd}}=W . The prediction x ~ θ ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)} is
constructed by first noising the rollout sample x ^ ( i ) \hat{x}^{(i)} to level t t and
then transporting the resulting state x ~ t ( i ) \tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)} back to r = 0 r=0 by Equation ( 21 ). Here ω ( i ) \omega^{(i)} is the signed reward coefficient used in the
general loss Equation ( 30 ), and | ω ( i ) | |\omega^{(i)}| is its magnitude. For methods whose
coefficient follows the advantage, a non-zero ω ( i ) \omega^{(i)} has the sign of A ( i ) A^{(i)} in the theoretical weighting (the implemented DiffusionNFT weighting
of § 5.5 can deviate), and a clipping gate may set it to zero. Self-regression. The first term does not depend on the reward. It
vanishes at x ~ θ ( i ) = x ~ reg ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)}=\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}}^{(i)} and grows with the distance from this
anchor, so its gradient moves the prediction back toward the anchor. This
self-regression term limits how far the reward-augmentation term can move the
prediction and therefore helps protect against reward hacking. The regression anchor is therefore chosen as a prediction that the policy
should stay close to. A common choice is the old policy’s prediction x ~ old ( i ) = X ⁡ [ x ~ t ( i ) , v old , 0 ] \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{old}}^{(i)}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)},\,v_{\mathrm{old}},\,0\big] , so that
the term penalises the deviation of the current prediction from that of the
policy used for sampling. Since x ~ θ ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)} is computed from x ~ t ( i ) \tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)} , the
anchor x ~ reg ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}}^{(i)} must be computed from the same x ~ t ( i ) \tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)} ; otherwise, the
difference x ~ θ ( i ) − x ~ reg ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)}-\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}}^{(i)} would mix a change of the velocity field with
a change of the state and would no longer measure the deviation that the term
is meant to bound. Reward augmentation. For fixed anchors, the second term contributes
a vector that does not depend on the current prediction and contains the
reward. Under gradient descent it moves x ~ θ ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)} along + ω ( i ) ​ ( x ~ rwd ( i ) − x ~ base ( i ) ) +\omega^{(i)}(\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd}}^{(i)}-\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}^{(i)}) , a displacement whose direction is set by the sign of ω ( i ) \omega^{(i)} and whose coefficient has
magnitude | ω ( i ) | |\omega^{(i)}| . The magnitude of the full term also depends on W rwd ( i ) W_{\mathrm{rwd}}^{(i)} and on the anchor displacement x ~ rwd ( i ) − x ~ base ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd}}^{(i)}-\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}^{(i)} . The rewarded anchor x ~ rwd ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd}}^{(i)} is usually the rollout sample, since
transporting x ~ t ( i ) \tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)} with the conditional velocity gives X ⁡ [ x ~ t ( i ) , v cond ( i ) , 0 ] = x ^ ( i ) X\big[\tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)},\,v_{\mathrm{cond}}^{(i)},\,0\big]=\hat{x}^{(i)} . This choice is natural because A ( i ) A^{(i)} is obtained by scoring x ^ ( i ) \hat{x}^{(i)} and provides no direct information about
other points. For methods whose coefficient follows the advantage, the sign of ω ( i ) \omega^{(i)} determines whether the prediction moves toward x ~ rwd ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd}}^{(i)} or
away from it. The other end, x ~ base ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}^{(i)} , is the baseline anchor. The displacement x ~ rwd ( i ) − x ~ base ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd}}^{(i)}-\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}^{(i)} points from the baseline anchor to the rewarded
anchor, so x ~ base ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}^{(i)} sets the reference point and the line along which the
reward acts. Admissible choices for x ~ base ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}^{(i)} include
the old policy’s prediction x ~ old ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{old}}^{(i)} and the
detached current prediction sg ⁡ ( x ~ θ ( i ) ) \operatorname{sg}(\tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)}) . In the RL methods, x ~ base ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}^{(i)} is also evaluated
at the state x ~ t ( i ) \tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)} from which x ~ θ ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)} is computed, so that the two
anchors define a displacement in the space on which the update acts. An intuitive example. Consider a group-advantage-based method with
the group-mean advantage of Equation ( 12 ) and a
group in which A ( 1 ) > A ( 2 ) > 0 > A ( 3 ) A^{(1)}>A^{(2)}>0>A^{(3)} . Members 1 1 and 2 2 both score
above the group mean, so ω ( 1 ) , ω ( 2 ) > 0 \omega^{(1)},\omega^{(2)}>0 and both predictions are
attracted toward their rewarded samples. Member 2 2 is not penalised for
scoring lower than member 1 1 ; it only receives a coefficient of smaller or equal magnitude, since | ω ( 1 ) | ≥ | ω ( 2 ) | |\omega^{(1)}|\geq|\omega^{(2)}| in AWM and in the theoretical DiffusionNFT
weighting below. Member 3 3 scores below the mean, so ω ( 3 ) < 0 \omega^{(3)}<0 and its
prediction is repelled away from x ~ rwd ( 3 ) \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd}}^{(3)} , in the direction x ~ base ( 3 ) − x ~ rwd ( 3 ) \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}^{(3)}-\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd}}^{(3)} . Whether a prediction is attracted or repelled is
therefore determined by the baseline-subtracted advantage A ( i ) A^{(i)} and not by
the raw reward; a sample with a high reward is still repelled if it scores
below its group mean. Recovering the loss. Compared with the DiffusionNFT decomposition (Example 4 below, Equation ( 35 )), there are three main
differences. First, the baseline anchor is the detached current prediction, so
one end of the reward displacement is fixed and the other moves with the
policy. The term is therefore recomputed at every step and is proportional to
the residual x ^ ( i ) − sg ⁡ ( x ~ θ ( i ) ) \hat{x}^{(i)}-\operatorname{sg}(\tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)}) instead of being a fixed displacement;
this is why AWM can be written as the single weighted square Equation ( 25 ).
Second, in Equation ( 32 ) the magnitude | ω ( i ) | = | A ( i ) | |\omega^{(i)}|=|A^{(i)}| is not clipped, whereas in
DiffusionNFT it saturates at 1 / β 1/\beta . Third, with W reg ( i ) = 0 W_{\mathrm{reg}}^{(i)}=0 the matching loss contains no self-regression term; this role is played by the
additional velocity-space proximal penalty ‖ v θ − v old ‖ 2 \left\lVert v_{\theta}-v_{\mathrm{old}}\right\rVert^{2} of
§ 3.2 , which under Equation ( 22 ) is a self-regression toward
the old policy’s prediction x ~ old ( i ) \tilde{x}^{(i)}_{\mathrm{old}} . Example 4 (Decoupling DiffusionNFT loss) . Applying the same decomposition to Equation ( 27 ) yields both terms of Equation ( 28 ). The two branches Equation ( 26 ) are affine in
the same current prediction, so ∂ x ~ θ + ∂ θ = β ​ ∂ x ~ θ ( i ) ∂ θ , ∂ x ~ θ − ∂ θ = − β ​ ∂ x ~ θ ( i ) ∂ θ . \frac{\partial\tilde{x}^{+}_{\theta}}{\partial\theta}=\beta\,\frac{\partial\tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)}}{\partial\theta},\qquad\frac{\partial\tilde{x}^{-}_{\theta}}{\partial\theta}=-\beta\,\frac{\partial\tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)}}{\partial\theta}. a single Jacobian. Since r ^ ( i ) = 1 2 + 1 2 ​ clip ⁡ ( A ( i ) , − 1 , 1 ) \hat{r}^{(i)}=\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\operatorname{clip}(A^{(i)},-1,1) , ∇ θ ℒ NFT ( i ) = 2 ​ β 2 t 2 ​ ( ∂ x ~ θ ( i ) ∂ θ ) ⊤ ​ [ ( x ~ θ ( i ) − x ~ base ( i ) ) − clip ⁡ ( A ( i ) , − 1 , 1 ) β ​ ( x ^ ( i ) − x ~ base ( i ) ) ] , \nabla_{\theta}\mathcal{L}^{(i)}_{\mathrm{NFT}}=\frac{2\beta^{2}}{t^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial\tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)}}{\partial\theta}\right)^{\!\!\top}\Big[\big(\tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)}-\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}^{(i)}\big)-\frac{\operatorname{clip}\big(A^{(i)},-1,1\big)}{\beta}\big(\hat{x}^{(i)}-\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}^{(i)}\big)\Big], (34) which matches Equation ( 28 ) term by term, with a self-regression

The corresponding anchors and weights are x ~ reg ( i ) = x ~ base ( i ) = X ⁡ [ x ~ t ( i ) , v old , 0 ] , x ~ rwd ( i ) = x ^ ( i ) , W reg ( i ) = W rwd ( i ) = β 2 t 2 , ω ( i ) = clip ⁡ ( A ( i ) , − 1 , 1 ) β . \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}}^{(i)}=\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}^{(i)}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t}^{(i)},\,v_{\mathrm{old}},\,0\big],\quad\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd}}^{(i)}=\hat{x}^{(i)},\quad W_{\mathrm{reg}}^{(i)}=W_{\mathrm{rwd}}^{(i)}=\frac{\beta^{2}}{t^{2}},\quad\omega^{(i)}=\frac{\operatorname{clip}\big(A^{(i)},-1,1\big)}{\beta}. (35) Both anchors coincide at the old policy’s clean prediction, so
DiffusionNFT regularises toward the point from which it measures the reward.
The rewarded sample is the rollout produced by the model. The two weights are
equal, which allows the published loss to be written as one fused square up to a detached term. The
signed reward coefficient ω ( i ) = clip ⁡ ( A ( i ) , − 1 , 1 ) / β \omega^{(i)}=\operatorname{clip}(A^{(i)},-1,1)/\beta has a
magnitude that increases with | A ( i ) | |A^{(i)}| and saturates at 1 / β 1/\beta because of
the clip. In comparison, methods with W reg ( i ) = 0 W_{\mathrm{reg}}^{(i)}=0 , such as AWM above, drop the self-regression
from the matching loss. Example 5 (Reward Augmentation for DMD and MeanFlow) . The decomposition also applies to reward-free distillation losses and
yields their decoupled form. Neither objective scores a group, so there is
no advantage and no index ( i ) (i) ; both move the policy toward the teacher.

A few-step policy instead outputs an average velocity u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , r ) u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r) . To evaluate Equation ( 30 ), the velocity in Equation ( 21 ) can be replaced by a velocity recovered from u θ u_{\theta} , and the anchors by the matching
predictions of u old u_{\mathrm{old}} or the teacher. Therefore, methods differ in which predictions and
anchors they use. A rollout sample x ^ ( i ) \hat{x}^{(i)} is already a sample and needs no
reconstruction, and the teacher prediction Equation ( 36 ) remains
instantaneous, so only x ~ θ \tilde{x}_{\theta} and the old-policy anchors need to be rebuilt. Building anchors with u-prediction. The direct substitution queries only the r = 0 r=0 edge of the domain { 0 ≤ r ≤ t ≤ 1 } \{0\leq r\leq t\leq 1\} of the flow map, x ~ θ , u = X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , 0 ) , 0 ] = x ~ t − t ​ u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , 0 ) . \tilde{x}_{\theta,\mathrm{u}}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,0),\,0\big]=\tilde{x}_{t}-t\,u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,0). (47) The policy velocity v θ ​ ( x ~ t , t ) v_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t) in § 4 is replaced by u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , 0 ) u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,0) , and the old policy is treated in the same way, x ~ old , u = X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u old ​ ( x ~ t , t , 0 ) , 0 ] = x ~ t − t ​ u old ​ ( x ~ t , t , 0 ) . \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{u}}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\mathrm{old}}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,0),\,0\big]=\tilde{x}_{t}-t\,u_{\mathrm{old}}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,0). (48) The current prediction of Equation ( 30 ) is then x ~ θ ← x ~ θ , u \tilde{x}_{\theta}\leftarrow\tilde{x}_{\theta,\mathrm{u}} , and
both the regression and the baseline anchor are x ~ old , u \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{u}} , or x ~ base = sg ⁡ ( x ~ θ , u ) \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}=\operatorname{sg}(\tilde{x}_{\theta,\mathrm{u}}) in the AWM case
where the baseline follows the policy. The Jacobian in Equation ( 28 ) is then ∂ x ~ θ , u / ∂ θ \partial\tilde{x}_{\theta,\mathrm{u}}/\partial\theta ,
so each update trains only u θ ​ ( ⋅ , t , 0 ) u_{\theta}(\cdot,t,0) . The sampler Equation ( 5 )
uses x ^ θ , r = x ^ θ , t − ( t − r ) ​ u θ ​ ( ⋅ , t , r ) \hat{x}_{\theta,r}=\hat{x}_{\theta,t}-(t-r)\,u_{\theta}(\cdot,t,r) with r r not
necessarily zero. Therefore, in u-prediction, the flow map is trained only on its r = 0 r=0 edge and leaves the entries with r ≠ 0 r\neq 0 , which the sampler uses, without a
gradient. Building anchors with v-prediction. In u-prediction, the instantaneous velocity is not used. Alternatively, Equation ( 30 ) keeps x ~ θ = X ⁡ [ x ~ t , v , 0 ] \tilde{x}_{\theta}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,v,\,0\big] , and the remaining question is
how to construct v v from a network that outputs an average velocity. Following
Example 5 , there are two constructions. Self-induced velocity. Equation ( 37 ) recovers the instantaneous
velocity from the average velocity itself, as in MeanFlowNFT, which applies
DiffusionNFT, regressed on the forward (noising) process, to an
average-velocity policy ( Huang et al., 2026b ) . The current prediction
adds the same detached J J to u θ u_{\theta} , which gives the v-prediction x ~ θ , v = X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u θ + J ⁡ [ u old , t , r ] , 0 ] = x ~ t − t ⁡ ( u θ + J ⁡ [ u old , t , r ] ) , \tilde{x}_{\theta,\mathrm{v}}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\theta}+J\big[u_{\mathrm{old}},\,t,\,r\big],\,0\big]=\tilde{x}_{t}-t\big(u_{\theta}+J\big[u_{\mathrm{old}},\,t,\,r\big]\big), (49) and the old policy gives the self-induced anchor Equation ( 38 ), x ~ ind = X ⁡ [ x ~ t , v ind , 0 ] = x ~ t − t ⁡ ( u old + J ⁡ [ u old , t , r ] ) , \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{ind}}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,v_{\mathrm{ind}},\,0\big]=\tilde{x}_{t}-t\big(u_{\mathrm{old}}+J\big[u_{\mathrm{old}},\,t,\,r\big]\big), (50) so that x ~ θ , v − x ~ ind = − t ⁡ ( u θ − u old ) \tilde{x}_{\theta,\mathrm{v}}-\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{ind}}=-t(u_{\theta}-u_{\mathrm{old}}) . Since J J is detached, Equation ( 41 ) still holds; J J shifts the prediction but does not change its Jacobian. The pair ( t , r ) (t,r) is the
input of the network and r r is resampled at every update, so every u θ ​ ( ⋅ , t , r ) u_{\theta}(\cdot,t,r) on the trajectory enters ∂ x ~ θ / ∂ θ \partial\tilde{x}_{\theta}/\partial\theta . The
loss itself is still evaluated at r = 0 r=0 ; r r enters only as a network input and through J J , and
does not change the level at which Equation ( 30 ) is evaluated. Auxiliary velocity. The instantaneous velocity can instead be
parameterised by a second network v ϕ v_{\phi} , fitted by Equation ( 9 ) to the
samples of the model, so that its population target is the marginal velocity of p θ p_{\theta} , v ϕ ​ ( x ~ t , t ) ≈ 𝔼 x ^ ∼ p θ , ϵ ​ [ ϵ − x ^ ∣ x ~ t ] , x ~ t = ( 1 − t ) ​ x ^ + t ​ ϵ . v_{\phi}(\tilde{x}_{t},t)\;\approx\;\mathbb{E}_{\,\hat{x}\sim p_{\theta},\,\epsilon}\big[\epsilon-\hat{x}\mid\tilde{x}_{t}\big],\qquad\tilde{x}_{t}=(1-t)\,\hat{x}+t\,\epsilon. (51) In the RL setting of § 3.2 , however, the rollouts are samples from p old p_{\mathrm{old}} and not from p θ p_{\theta} , so the target is the marginal velocity of p old p_{\mathrm{old}} , v ϕ ​ ( x ~ t , t ) ≈ 𝔼 x ^ ∼ p old , ϵ ​ [ ϵ − x ^ ∣ x ~ t ] , x ~ t = ( 1 − t ) ​ x ^ + t ​ ϵ . v_{\phi}(\tilde{x}_{t},t)\;\approx\;\mathbb{E}_{\,\hat{x}\sim p_{\mathrm{old}},\,\epsilon}\big[\epsilon-\hat{x}\mid\tilde{x}_{t}\big],\qquad\tilde{x}_{t}=(1-t)\,\hat{x}+t\,\epsilon. (52) Its clean prediction x ~ aux = X ⁡ [ x ~ t , v ϕ , 0 ] \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{aux}}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,v_{\phi},\,0\big] is detached, like x ~ ind \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{ind}} , and
serves only as an anchor. The current prediction cannot be computed from v ϕ v_{\phi} , since ∂ x ~ θ / ∂ θ \partial\tilde{x}_{\theta}/\partial\theta would then vanish and the reward would not affect
the policy. It is therefore computed from u θ u_{\theta} , e.g., as x ~ θ , u \tilde{x}_{\theta,\mathrm{u}} , x ~ θ , v \tilde{x}_{\theta,\mathrm{v}} , or the J J -free ( t , r ) (t,r) query X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , r ) , 0 ] X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r),\,0\big] , i.e., the
transport of x ~ t \tilde{x}_{t} to the clean end with the average velocity queried at ( t , r ) (t,r) , which has the same Jacobian as x ~ θ , v \tilde{x}_{\theta,\mathrm{v}} . Its old-policy counterpart is x ~ ind \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{ind}} with J J dropped, x ~ ind | J = 0 = X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u old ​ ( x ~ t , t , r ) , 0 ] {\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{ind}}|_{J=0}}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\mathrm{old}}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r),\,0\big] . The self-induced construction requires a Jacobian-vector product with a
stop-gradient; the auxiliary construction requires a second network and
optimiser that must follow a moving target. Within the framework, a few-step method therefore does not introduce a new loss; it specifies how the
four points of Equation ( 30 ) are computed and which entries u θ ​ ( ⋅ , t , r ) u_{\theta}(\cdot,t,r) of the flow map receive a gradient through ∂ x ~ θ / ∂ θ \partial\tilde{x}_{\theta}/\partial\theta . Table 1 summarises the three

Example 6 (DiffusionNFT with u-prediction) . only r = 0 r=0 . This is DiffusionNFT evaluated on the r = 0 r=0 edge ( t , 0 ) (t,0) . Example 7 (DiffusionNFT with v-prediction) . The decomposition Equation ( 35 ) carries over, and the self-induced
construction below keeps x ~ reg = x ~ base \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}}=\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}} . The remaining choice is how to recover
the instantaneous velocity used in Equation ( 21 ) from an average-velocity
network, and Equation ( 49 )–( 52 ) give two ways to do so.
The first is to use the self-induced velocity v ind = u old + J ⁡ [ u old , t , r ] v_{\mathrm{ind}}=u_{\mathrm{old}}+J\big[u_{\mathrm{old}},\,t,\,r\big] , which adds the Jacobian-vector term J ⁡ [ u old , t , r ] J\big[u_{\mathrm{old}},\,t,\,r\big] to every prediction of the policy and of the old-policy
anchor, as in Equation ( 49 )–( 50 ); this construction gives
the MeanFlowNFT -style variant ( Huang et al., 2026b ) . The second
is to fit an auxiliary network v ϕ v_{\phi} to the marginal
velocity of the rollouts, Equation ( 51 )–( 52 ), and
compute the baseline as x ~ aux \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{aux}} , which leads to DMDNFT . In both cases the current prediction x ~ θ \tilde{x}_{\theta} is computed from u θ u_{\theta} , since otherwise ∂ x ~ θ / ∂ θ \partial\tilde{x}_{\theta}/\partial\theta would vanish. MeanFlowNFT queries the network at ( t , r ) (t,r) with 0 ≤ r ≤ t 0\leq r\leq t , so
every prediction of the policy is a v-prediction. Following prior
directional-difference flow-map work ( Nie et al., 2026 ; Gu et al., 2026 ) , the
MeanFlowNFT construction evaluates one derivative on the frozen old policy and
shares it between the current and the old policy ( Huang et al., 2026b ) , which gives the four points x ~ θ \displaystyle\tilde{x}_{\theta} = x ~ θ , v = X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u θ + J ⁡ [ u old , t , r ] , 0 ] , \displaystyle=\tilde{x}_{\theta,\mathrm{v}}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\theta}+J\big[u_{\mathrm{old}},\,t,\,r\big],\,0\big], (56) x ~ reg = x ~ base \displaystyle\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}}=\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}} = x ~ ind = X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u old + J ⁡ [ u old , t , r ] , 0 ] \displaystyle=\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{ind}}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\mathrm{old}}+J\big[u_{\mathrm{old}},\,t,\,r\big],\,0\big] x ~ rwd \displaystyle\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd}} = x ^ . \displaystyle=\hat{x}. Starting from the two squares of Equation ( 30 ), ℒ reg \displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{reg}} = W reg ​ ‖ X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u θ + J ⁡ [ u old , t , r ] , 0 ] − X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u old + J ⁡ [ u old , t , r ] , 0 ] ‖ 2 \displaystyle=W_{\mathrm{reg}}\left\lVert X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\theta}+J\big[u_{\mathrm{old}},\,t,\,r\big],\,0\big]-X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\mathrm{old}}+J\big[u_{\mathrm{old}},\,t,\,r\big],\,0\big]\right\rVert^{2} (57) = W reg ​ ‖ X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , r ) , 0 ] − X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u old ​ ( x ~ t , t , r ) , 0 ] ‖ 2 , \displaystyle=W_{\mathrm{reg}}\left\lVert X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r),\,0\big]-X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\mathrm{old}}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r),\,0\big]\right\rVert^{2}, ℒ rwd \displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{rwd}} = W rwd ​ ‖ X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u θ + J ⁡ [ u old , t , r ] , 0 ] − sg ⁡ ( X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u θ + J ⁡ [ u old , t , r ] , 0 ] + g v ) ‖ 2 \displaystyle=W_{\mathrm{rwd}}\left\lVert X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\theta}+J\big[u_{\mathrm{old}},\,t,\,r\big],\,0\big]-\operatorname{sg}\big(X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\theta}+J\big[u_{\mathrm{old}},\,t,\,r\big],\,0\big]+g_{\mathrm{v}}\big)\right\rVert^{2} (58) = W rwd ​ ‖ X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , r ) , 0 ] − sg ⁡ ( X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , r ) , 0 ] + g v ) ‖ 2 . \displaystyle=W_{\mathrm{rwd}}\left\lVert X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r),\,0\big]-\operatorname{sg}\big(X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r),\,0\big]+g_{\mathrm{v}}\big)\right\rVert^{2}. between the two copies of the current prediction.

with v ϕ v_{\phi} fitted online to the old-policy rollouts by Equation ( 52 ). The current prediction is still computed from u θ u_{\theta} ,

reward-gradient steps in x x -space, in the ascent and in the descent direction.

only at r = 0 r=0 . Example 9 (ReFL as Few-step DiffusionOPSD) . Reward feedback learning (ReFL) backpropagates the gradient of a
differentiable reward through a single late denoising step. Classic ReFL stops

Apart from W rwd W_{\mathrm{rwd}} and the velocity penalty, a step requires one detached rollout, two forward
passes of u old u_{\mathrm{old}} (at r r and at r = 0 r=0 ), one reward
gradient, and one forward and backward pass of u θ u_{\theta} ; J J cancels, because it
shifts both ends of the displacement equally, and is therefore dropped. With J J dropped and r = 0 r=0 we have x ~ ind = x ~ old , u \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{ind}}=\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{u}} , which is the u-form. At a generic r r , only the
current prediction and its anchor use ( t , r ) (t,r) ; the decoder and the reward are
still evaluated at the clean prediction x ~ old , u \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{u}} , while the resulting
displacement trains u θ ​ ( ⋅ , t , r ) u_{\theta}(\cdot,t,r) . 5 Implementation The framework of § 4 is implemented as one base trainer whose
method step runs one outer iteration. Each trainer of
§§ 5.3 – 5.6 is a subclass that overrides
a few stage methods, and the instantaneous-velocity estimator of § 4.3 , v-ind or v-aux , is a separate object passed to the trainer.
Here v-ind uses the
MeanFlow identity with the shared old-policy correction J J to construct an
instantaneous velocity from an average velocity, whereas v-aux uses an auxiliary velocity to
approximate the instantaneous velocity of the old-policy rollout distribution p old p_{\mathrm{old}} (which DMD takes to be p θ p_{\theta} ), as in Equation ( 52 ). Stages 1 and 2 below operate on groups, since they
produce and score the K K members of a prompt, and keep the index ( i ) (i) as in
§§ 3 – 4.3 . From Stage 3 onward the framework acts
on one member at a time. The prediction, the
three anchors and the three weights are per-sample quantities; the index ( i ) (i) is omitted in the equations and in the loss terms ℒ reg \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{reg}} , ℒ rwd \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{rwd}} , and becomes the leading batch axis in code. 5.1 A General Training Step One outer iteration fixes an old policy u old u_{\mathrm{old}} , rolls it out, scores the
rollouts, and then takes one gradient step on the mean over n it n_{\mathrm{it}} queries of ℒ ⁡ ( θ ) = 𝔼 ⁡ [ W reg ​ mean sp ​ [ ( x ~ θ − x ~ reg ) 2 ] + W rwd ​ mean sp ​ [ ( x ~ θ − sg ⁡ ( x ~ θ + g ) ) 2 ] + λ KL ​ ℒ KL ] , \mathcal{L}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}\Big[W_{\mathrm{reg}}\,\operatorname{mean}_{\mathrm{sp}}\!\left[\big(\tilde{x}_{\theta}-\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}}\big)^{2}\right]+W_{\mathrm{rwd}}\,\operatorname{mean}_{\mathrm{sp}}\!\left[\big(\tilde{x}_{\theta}-\operatorname{sg}(\tilde{x}_{\theta}+g)\big)^{2}\right]+\lambda_{\mathrm{KL}}\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{KL}}\Big], (80) where x ^ ( i ) = Rollout ​ ( ϵ ( i ) , u old , c ) \hat{x}^{(i)}=\textsc{Rollout}(\epsilon^{(i)},u_{\mathrm{old}},c) is a detached rollout of
prompt c c . The per-sample mean mean sp ⁡ [ ⋅ ] \operatorname{mean}_{\mathrm{sp}}\!\left[\cdot\right] rescales every weight W W by 1 / N 1/N relative to the squared norms of § 4.2 ; this constant is
absorbed into the learning rate. ℒ KL \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{KL}} is the velocity penalty of Stage 4 with weight λ KL \lambda_{\mathrm{KL}} , evaluated on the same method-specific query. The training workflow. One outer iteration consists of five
operations, executed in order; the four method-specific stages of
§ 1 are operations 1, 2, 4 and the loss inside 4, while
operations 3 and 5 are shared bookkeeping. (i) Rollout : the old policy u old u_{\mathrm{old}} samples K K members per
prompt with the sampler of Stage 1, and the endpoints x ^ ( i ) \hat{x}^{(i)} are detached. (ii) Reward and advantage : each endpoint is decoded and scored, R ( i ) = R ⁡ ( 𝒟 ⁡ ( x ^ ( i ) ) , c ) R^{(i)}=R\big(\mathcal{D}(\hat{x}^{(i)}),c\big) , and Stage 2 turns the rewards into the
advantage A ( i ) A^{(i)} and the mixing weight r ^ ( i ) \hat{r}^{(i)} ( Equation ( 83 ), Equation ( 84 )). (iii) Auxiliary fit : a trainer that uses the auxiliary velocity v ϕ v_{\phi} first fits it to these rollouts with the flow-matching loss Equation ( 88 ) for n fm n_{\mathrm{fm}} steps, before the policy is updated;
the other trainers skip this step. (iv) Training loss : n it n_{\mathrm{it}} inner iterations each draw
a query (Stage 3 (i) ), compute the prediction, anchors and coefficients
(Stage 3 (ii) – (iv) ), and accumulate the gradient of Equation ( 80 )
(Stage 4); one optimiser step is taken on their mean. (v) Refresh : u old u_{\mathrm{old}} becomes the EMA of u θ u_{\theta} , and the next
iteration rolls out with it. In code, this is the method step of the base trainer in Section 5.1 , one block per operation: rollout , advantage , estimator . fit , the loop over queries with anchors , weights and loss , and refresh_old . Each Query object is a
view of the batch for one method-specific training query; fields it does not
set, such as the advantage, are read from the batch. A trainer of §§ 5.3 – 5.6 is a subclass
that implements anchors and weights , and
overrides another hook only where it changes that stage ( Table 2 ). anchors returns the prediction and three anchors of Equation ( 90 ), with a tuple of rewarded anchors for FewStepOPSD; weights returns the detached coefficients, with ω \omega signed and a tuple for FewStepOPSD, and loss is shared by
every trainer except FewStepOPSD and ReFL, which override it. The named methods of
§ 6 other than ReFL differ from their family only in the estimator they use. Code 1: The base trainer: one outer iteration ⬇ @dataclass class Points : # prediction and anchors of Eq. The method-specific quantities μ θ \mu_{\theta} , ρ ¯ GRPO \bar{\rho}_{\mathrm{GRPO}} , ρ AWM \rho_{\mathrm{AWM}} , χ GRPO \chi_{\mathrm{GRPO}} , χ AWM \chi_{\mathrm{AWM}} , σ k \sigma_{\mathrm{k}} , τ AWM \tau_{\mathrm{AWM}} , a ± a_{\pm} , γ ± \gamma_{\pm} and c adv c_{\mathrm{adv}} are defined in
§§ 5.2 – 5.5 . 5.2 Introduction to the Key Stages This subsection describes, for each stage of Section 5.1 , the default
computation, its cost, and the choices that the framework determines. Stage 1: the rollout operator Let Rollout denote the sampler. We distinguish two cases. (a) Sample-independent rollout. For every trainer except the FewStepOPSD
family, the group members of a prompt are i.i.d. draws: x ^ ( i ) = Rollout ( ϵ ( i ) , u old , c ) , ϵ ( i ) ∼ iid 𝒩 ( 0 , I ) , i = 1 , … , K , \hat{x}^{(i)}\;=\;\textsc{Rollout}\big(\epsilon^{(i)},\,u_{\mathrm{old}},\,c\big),\qquad\epsilon^{(i)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{iid}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,I),\quad i=1,\dots,K, (81) i.e. the only stochasticity is the initial noise (and, for the stochastic
sampler below, the per-step renoise). Members do not interact. The sampler uses the old policy u old u_{\mathrm{old}} and not u θ u_{\theta} , as indicated by
the argument of Equation ( 81 ). One old policy is fixed per
outer iteration, so that the rewarded endpoint x ^ ( i ) \hat{x}^{(i)} , the regression
anchor and the baseline anchor all refer to the same distribution, as required
in § 4.2 , where x ~ reg ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}}^{(i)} and x ~ base ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}^{(i)} must be evaluated
at the state from which x ~ θ ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\theta}^{(i)} is computed. If the samples were drawn from u θ u_{\theta} , the reward would be evaluated on a distribution different from the one
described by the anchors, and A ( i ) A^{(i)} would not be the advantage of the
policy that defines the anchors. (b) Reward-coupled rollout. For reward-coupled rollout, FewStepOPSD extends
the rollout with the reward, { x ^ ( i ) , x ~ old u , ( i ) , x ~ ± ( i ) } i = 1 K = Rollout ​ ( ϵ ( i ) , u old , R , c ) . \big\{\hat{x}^{(i)},\,\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{old}}^{\mathrm{u},(i)},\,\tilde{x}^{(i)}_{\pm}\big\}_{i=1}^{K}\;=\;\textsc{Rollout}\big(\epsilon^{(i)},\,u_{\mathrm{old}},\,R,\,c\big). (82) The members remain i.i.d.; the reward does not couple different members.
For each member, the sampler of Equation ( 81 ) runs unchanged.
The reward-coupled construction then applies Equation ( 62 ) from the r = 0 r=0 prediction x ~ old u , ( i ) \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{old}}^{\mathrm{u},(i)} and transfers
the resulting displacements to the anchor by Equation ( 70 ), giving x ~ ± ( i ) \tilde{x}^{(i)}_{\pm} together with the endpoint. In code, rollout defaults to (a) with the deterministic
sampler; FewStepGRPO overrides it with the stochastic sampler of § 5.3 , keeping the
transitions, and FewStepOPSD appends the reward-coupled construction of (b). Stage 2: reward and group advantage The generic advantage Equation ( 12 ) takes both the mean and the
standard deviation over the group; the implementation changes the population of the standard deviation.
With R ( i ) = R ⁡ ( 𝒟 ⁡ ( x ^ ( i ) ) , c ) R^{(i)}=R\big(\mathcal{D}(\hat{x}^{(i)}),c\big) the
endpoint reward ( 𝒟 \mathcal{D} the VAE decoder), the advantage is A ( i ) = R ( i ) − R ¯ c std ⁡ ( { R } ℬ ) + 10 − 4 , R ¯ c = 1 | H c | ​ ∑ R ∈ H c R , {\;A^{(i)}=\frac{R^{(i)}-\bar{R}_{c}}{\operatorname{std}\big(\{R\}_{\mathcal{B}}\big)+10^{-4}},\qquad\bar{R}_{c}=\frac{1}{|H_{c}|}\sum_{R\in H_{c}}R,\;} (83) where H c H_{c} is the set of rewards of prompt c c in the current outer iteration and { R } ℬ \{R\}_{\mathcal{B}} is the current global batch gathered across all ranks. The numerator and denominator use different
populations. The per-prompt mean reduces the effect of prompt difficulty, and
the global standard deviation avoids division by a near-zero value when all
rewards in a group are equal. The advantage is then compressed into a bounded mixing weight, generalising Equation ( 18 ) by replacing the unit clip range with a tunable c adv c_{\mathrm{adv}} : r ^ ( i ) = clip ⁡ ( A ( i ) , − c adv , c adv ) 2 ​ c adv + 1 2 ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] . {\;\hat{r}^{(i)}\;=\;\frac{\operatorname{clip}\big(A^{(i)},\,-c_{\mathrm{adv}},\,c_{\mathrm{adv}}\big)}{2\,c_{\mathrm{adv}}}+\frac{1}{2}\;\in[0,1].\;} (84) Here r ^ → 1 \hat{r}\to 1 means “far above the prompt’s mean”, r ^ → 0 \hat{r}\to 0 “far
below”, r ^ = 1 2 \hat{r}=\tfrac{1}{2} “average”. This [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] form distinguishes the
NFT/OPSD families from FewStepGRPO and FewStepAWM, which use clip ⁡ ( A , − c adv , c adv ) \operatorname{clip}(A,-c_{\mathrm{adv}},c_{\mathrm{adv}}) as a signed multiplier (§ 3.2 ); A A in Equation ( 97 ) and Equation ( 103 ) denotes this clipped value. In code, advantage returns ( A , r ^ ) (A,\hat{r}) ; ReFL overrides it
with r ^ ≡ 1 \hat{r}\equiv 1 . Stage 3: anchors and weights The trainers differ mainly in this stage. It receives the detached rollout and
returns the current prediction, the anchors and the detached coefficients;
FewStepOPSD returns branch-indexed tuples for its rewarded anchors and ω \omega .
We describe it in four parts, namely (i) the query levels, (ii) the two ways to
compute the clean prediction, (iii) the two estimators of the instantaneous
velocity that these predictions require, and (iv) the weights. (i) The training query ( t , r ) (t,r) . By default, a fresh pair ( t , r ) (t,r) is drawn at every inner iteration;
“ r r is resampled at every update” in § 4.3 refers to this draw: t t is
drawn from a selectable law (the maximum of two uniforms or a logit-normal), r | t ∼ U ⁡ ( 0 , t ) r\mid t\sim U(0,t) , and a fixed fraction of the batch is set to r = t r=t and to r = 0 r=0 . The three cases play different roles. For r = t r=t the interval has zero
length and J ≡ 0 J\equiv 0 , so the network query is instantaneous and the loss
reduces to its instantaneous-velocity form; r = 0 r=0 trains the one-step generator and gives J J its
largest prefactor t − r t-r ; the generic case lets ∂ x ~ θ / ∂ θ \partial\tilde{x}_{\theta}/\partial\theta reach u θ ​ ( ⋅ , t , r ) u_{\theta}(\cdot,t,r) for every r r in between, as required by the second
column of Table 1 . (ii) The u- and v-predictions. Except for the replayed transition score of FewStepGRPO, the implemented reward
losses evaluate Equation ( 21 ) at the clean level r = 0 r=0 , but the network
outputs the average velocity u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , r ) u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r) instead of the instantaneous
velocity required by the map. Using u θ u_{\theta} directly gives the u-form: x ~ θ = x ~ θ , u = X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , 0 ) , 0 ] (u-form) . {\;\tilde{x}_{\theta}=\tilde{x}_{\theta,\mathrm{u}}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,0),\,0\big]\;}\qquad\text{(u-form)}. (85) Adding the detached correction of Equation ( 37 ) gives the
v-form; since the prediction carries the gradient, v-ind builds it with the self-induced velocity,
i.e., x ~ θ = x ~ θ , v = X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , r ) + J ⁡ [ u old , t , r ] , 0 ] (v-form, v-ind ) . {\;\tilde{x}_{\theta}=\tilde{x}_{\theta,\mathrm{v}}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r)+J\big[u_{\mathrm{old}},\,t,\,r\big],\,0\big]\;}\qquad\text{(v-form, {v-ind})}. (86) As in Equation ( 37 ), J J is computed from u old u_{\mathrm{old}} ,
so that a single estimate can be shared by all predictions, as required
in (iii) . Since J J is detached, by Equation ( 41 ) the Jacobian of the
v-form x ~ θ \tilde{x}_{\theta} is that of X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , r ) , 0 ] X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r),\,0\big] . For the anchors x ~ base \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}} , x ~ reg \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}} and x ~ rwd \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd}} ,
there are two alternatives in v-form, namely v-aux and v-ind . (iii) Estimating the instantaneous velocity. There are two estimators
of the instantaneous velocity, following MeanFlow (a.k.a. v-ind ) and DMD (a.k.a. v-aux ), respectively. Self-induced v ind v_{\mathrm{ind}} , with shared J J ( v-ind ). The v-form needs J J only as a detached vector. The directional-difference estimate, for a step

flow matching on the current rollout pool, ℒ aux = mean sp ⁡ [ ( v ϕ ​ ( x ~ t , t ) − v cond ) 2 ] , x ~ t = AddNoise ​ ( x ^ , t ) , \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{aux}}=\operatorname{mean}_{\mathrm{sp}}\!\left[\big(v_{\phi}(\tilde{x}_{t},t)-v_{\mathrm{cond}}\big)^{2}\right],\qquad\tilde{x}_{t}=\textsc{AddNoise}(\hat{x},t), (88) running n fm n_{\mathrm{fm}} steps of Equation ( 88 ) per outer iteration,
before the policy update. The auxiliary-to-policy optimiser-step ratio is n fm : 1 n_{\mathrm{fm}}:1 ( n fm = 5 n_{\mathrm{fm}}=5 in
