The ∅ \varnothing cases correspond to the two None checks, so every trainer except FewStepOPSD and ReFL runs the same loss regardless of the anchors it defines. In addition to Equation ( 90 ), an implemented loss may include a velocity
penalty against a frozen reference with its own weight, which
encourages the policy to stay close to the reference. This penalty is a separate term and
not an additional anchor. The average-velocity
reference u ref u_{\mathrm{ref}} is the initial pre-RL policy and remains fixed
throughout training.
For an endpoint-renoise query the KL-u penalty is ℒ KL u = mean sp ⁡ [ ( u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , r ) − u ref ​ ( x ~ t , t , r ) ) 2 ] . \mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{u}}_{\mathrm{KL}}=\operatorname{mean}_{\mathrm{sp}}\!\left[\big(u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r)-u_{\mathrm{ref}}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r)\big)^{2}\right]. (92) When the reference is an instantaneous velocity, here the teacher v real v_{\mathrm{real}} , it cannot be compared with u θ u_{\theta} directly for r ≠ t r\neq t . We therefore
convert it to an average-velocity target as in MeanFlow and regress u θ u_{\theta} onto
it (the KL-v penalty), ℒ KL v = mean sp ⁡ [ ( u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , r ) − ( v real ​ ( x ~ t , t ) − J ⁡ [ u old , t , r ] ) ) 2 ] . \mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathrm{KL}}=\operatorname{mean}_{\mathrm{sp}}\!\left[\Big(u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r)-\big(v_{\mathrm{real}}(\tilde{x}_{t},t)-J\big[u_{\mathrm{old}},\,t,\,r\big]\big)\Big)^{2}\right]. (93) 5.3 FewStepGRPO: Replaying Stored Trajectories FewStepGRPO is our replay-based surrogate adaptation of FlowGRPO ( Liu et al., 2025a ) . Stochastic sampler. The stochastic sampler generalises Equation ( 11 ). Each step first moves
from t j t_{j} to a level s j ≤ t j + 1 s_{j}\leq t_{j+1} , drawn uniformly from the remaining grid
levels, and then adds noise to return to level t j + 1 t_{j+1} on the linear path, x ^ s j = X ⁡ [ x ^ t j , u old ​ ( x ^ t j , t j , s j ) , s j ] , x ^ t j + 1 = 1 − t j + 1 1 − s j ​ x ^ s j + τ j ​ ϵ ′ , ϵ ′ ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( 0 , I ) , \hat{x}_{s_{j}}=X\big[\hat{x}_{t_{j}},\,u_{\mathrm{old}}(\hat{x}_{t_{j}},t_{j},s_{j}),\,s_{j}\big],\qquad\hat{x}_{t_{j+1}}=\frac{1-t_{j+1}}{1-s_{j}}\,\hat{x}_{s_{j}}+\tau_{j}\,\epsilon^{\prime},\qquad\epsilon^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I), (94) where the leading coefficient matches the clean component at level t j + 1 t_{j+1} and τ j \tau_{j} matches the noise variance, τ j 2 = t j + 1 2 − ( 1 − t j + 1 1 − s j ) 2 ​ s j 2 \tau_{j}^{2}=t_{j+1}^{2}-\big(\tfrac{1-t_{j+1}}{1-s_{j}}\big)^{2}s_{j}^{2} ,
which is non-negative if and only if s j ≤ t j + 1 s_{j}\leq t_{j+1} .
In Equation ( 94 ), s j s_{j} is the level at which the flow map is queried and t j + 1 t_{j+1} is the target level of the step; in the
notation of Equation ( 5 ), the flow map is thus queried with lower
endpoint s j s_{j} rather than r = t j + 1 r=t_{j+1} , and r = t j + 1 r=t_{j+1} denotes the target
level of the transition. Equation ( 11 ) is the
case s j = 0 s_{j}=0 , with t ′ t^{\prime} in place of t j + 1 t_{j+1} . Replay-trajectory query. Unlike endpoint-renoise trainers, it retains the M step M_{\mathrm{step}} transitions of the stochastic rollout, replays one stored ( x t , x r , t , r ) (x_{t},x_{r},t,r) per inner
iteration, and redraws only the flow-map query level s s , with the law of r r in
Stage 3 (i) . The redrawn s s need not equal the grid level s j s_{j} used
at rollout time; Section B.2 shows that the scored kernel is
unaffected by this choice at zero drift.
Thus n it = M step n_{\mathrm{it}}=M_{\mathrm{step}} , one endpoint advantage supervises
all transitions of a member, and the differentiated quantity is the mean μ θ \mu_{\theta} of the scoring kernel at level r r instead of a clean prediction. Scoring kernel. FewStepGRPO scores x r x_{r} with an Euler–Maruyama Gaussian, which reads μ θ \displaystyle\mu_{\theta} : = α t ​ x t − b t ​ ( u θ ​ ( x t , t , s ) + J ) , \displaystyle:=\alpha_{t}\,x_{t}-b_{t}\,\big(u_{\theta}(x_{t},t,s)+J\big), (95) μ old \displaystyle\mu_{\mathrm{old}} : = α t ​ x t − b t ​ ( u old ​ ( x t , t , s ) + J ) , \displaystyle:=\alpha_{t}\,x_{t}-b_{t}\,\big(u_{\mathrm{old}}(x_{t},t,s)+J\big), α t \displaystyle\alpha_{t} : = 1 − ς t 2 2 ​ t Δ t , b t := ( 1 + ς t 2 ​ ( 1 − t ) 2 ​ t ) Δ t , σ k := ς t Δ ​ t . \displaystyle:=1-\frac{\varsigma_{t}^{2}}{2t}\,\Delta t,\qquad b_{t}:=\Big(1+\frac{\varsigma_{t}^{2}(1-t)}{2t}\Big)\Delta t,\qquad\sigma_{\mathrm{k}}:=\varsigma_{t}\sqrt{\Delta t}. Here Δ ​ t = t − r \Delta t=t-r , α t \alpha_{t} and b t b_{t} are the Euler–Maruyama
coefficients, σ k \sigma_{\mathrm{k}} is the kernel width, ς t \varsigma_{t} is the noise scale of the scoring kernel,
and both means use the same detached correction J = J ⁡ [ u old , t , s ] J=J\big[u_{\mathrm{old}},\,t,\,s\big] evaluated on the replay trajectory with lower endpoint s s . The
definition of ς t \varsigma_{t} that avoids the singularity at t 0 = 1 t_{0}=1 , the trajectory correction and the SDE derivation are
deferred to Section B.2 . Anchors and weights. The tempered (per-element-averaged) scoring ratio and its detached clipping gate are,
explicitly, ρ ¯ GRPO \displaystyle\bar{\rho}_{\mathrm{GRPO}} : = exp ⁡ [ mean sp ⁡ [ ( μ old − x r ) 2 ] − mean sp ⁡ [ ( μ θ − x r ) 2 ] 2 ​ σ k 2 ] , \displaystyle:=\exp\!\left[\frac{\operatorname{mean}_{\mathrm{sp}}\!\left[(\mu_{\mathrm{old}}-x_{r})^{2}\right]-\operatorname{mean}_{\mathrm{sp}}\!\left[(\mu_{\theta}-x_{r})^{2}\right]}{2\sigma_{\mathrm{k}}^{2}}\right], (96) χ GRPO \displaystyle\chi_{\mathrm{GRPO}} : = [ ( A > 0 ∧ ρ ¯ GRPO ≤ 1 + ε clip ) ∨ ( A < 0 ∧ ρ ¯ GRPO ≥ 1 − ε clip ) ] . \displaystyle:=\mathbf{1}\!\left[\big(A>0\ \wedge\ \bar{\rho}_{\mathrm{GRPO}}\leq 1+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{clip}}\big)\ \vee\ \big(A<0\ \wedge\ \bar{\rho}_{\mathrm{GRPO}}\geq 1-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{clip}}\big)\right]. Thus χ GRPO = 0 \chi_{\mathrm{GRPO}}=0 when A = 0 A=0 . The quantity ρ ¯ GRPO \bar{\rho}_{\mathrm{GRPO}} is a surrogate scoring ratio rather than a rollout
importance weight. on the matching residual of the model. It has no explicit self-regression term,
and in MeanFlowAWM the reward displacement is measured from the detached current prediction. Rollout and prediction. Stage 1 uses the deterministic rollout, n it = n ts n_{\mathrm{it}}=n_{\mathrm{ts}} ,
and every inner iteration draws a fresh ( t , r , ϵ ) (t,r,\epsilon) , re-noises the
rewarded endpoint and computes the v-prediction Equation ( 86 ), x ~ t = AddNoise ​ ( x ^ , t ) , x ~ θ = x ~ θ , v = X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , r ) + J ⁡ [ u old , t , r ] , 0 ] , \tilde{x}_{t}=\textsc{AddNoise}(\hat{x},t),\qquad\tilde{x}_{\theta}=\tilde{x}_{\theta,\mathrm{v}}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r)+J\big[u_{\mathrm{old}},\,t,\,r\big],\,0\big], (98) so that, by Equation ( 22 ), x ~ θ − x ^ = − t ⁡ ( u θ − ( v cond − J ⁡ [ u old , t , r ] ) ) , v cond = ϵ − x ^ . \tilde{x}_{\theta}-\hat{x}=-t\Big(u_{\theta}-\big(v_{\mathrm{cond}}-J\big[u_{\mathrm{old}},\,t,\,r\big]\big)\Big),\qquad v_{\mathrm{cond}}=\epsilon-\hat{x}. (99) Thus u θ u_{\theta} regresses onto v cond − J v_{\mathrm{cond}}-J , the MeanFlow target Equation ( 8 ) with the conditional velocity in place of the teacher. MeanFlowAWM anchors and weights. The method uses the rollout endpoint as
the rewarded anchor, no explicit self-regression, and the current prediction as baseline: x ~ rwd = x ^ \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd}}=\hat{x} , x ~ reg = ∅ \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}}=\varnothing , x ~ base = sg ⁡ ( x ~ θ ) \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}=\operatorname{sg}(\tilde{x}_{\theta}) . Besides the shared J J in x ~ θ \tilde{x}_{\theta} , the old policy
enters only through the weights, via the shared- J J prediction of
Stage 3 (iii) , x ~ ind = X ⁡ [ x ~ t , u old ​ ( x ~ t , t , r ) + J ⁡ [ u old , t , r ] , 0 ] , x ~ θ − x ~ ind = − t ⁡ ( u θ − u old ) . \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{ind}}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\mathrm{old}}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r)+J\big[u_{\mathrm{old}},\,t,\,r\big],\,0\big],\qquad\tilde{x}_{\theta}-\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{ind}}=-t\big(u_{\theta}-u_{\mathrm{old}}\big). (100) The weights reuse the quadratic-exponential form of
§ 5.3 , but not its probabilistic interpretation: ρ AWM \displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{AWM}} : = exp ⁡ [ mean sp ⁡ [ ( x ~ ind − x ^ ) 2 ] − mean sp ⁡ [ ( x ~ θ − x ^ ) 2 ] 2 ​ τ AWM 2 ] , \displaystyle:=\exp\!\left[\frac{\operatorname{mean}_{\mathrm{sp}}\!\left[(\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{ind}}-\hat{x})^{2}\right]-\operatorname{mean}_{\mathrm{sp}}\!\left[(\tilde{x}_{\theta}-\hat{x})^{2}\right]}{2\tau_{\mathrm{AWM}}^{2}}\right], (101) χ AWM \displaystyle\chi_{\mathrm{AWM}} : = [ ( A > 0 ∧ ρ AWM ≤ 1 + ε clip ) ∨ ( A < 0 ∧ ρ AWM ≥ 1 − ε clip ) ] . \displaystyle:=\mathbf{1}\!\left[\big(A>0\ \wedge\ \rho_{\mathrm{AWM}}\leq 1+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{clip}}\big)\ \vee\ \big(A<0\ \wedge\ \rho_{\mathrm{AWM}}\geq 1-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{clip}}\big)\right]. kernel determines this scale. The choice τ AWM = t \tau_{\mathrm{AWM}}=t would cancel
the factor t 2 t^{2} in both residuals; when the floor is active, the factor t 2 / τ AWM 2 t^{2}/\tau_{\mathrm{AWM}}^{2} remains: ∂ ℒ ∂ u θ = ρ AWM ​ χ AWM ​ A N ​ t 2 τ AWM 2 ​ ( u θ − ( v cond − J ⁡ [ u old , t , r ] ) ) \frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial u_{\theta}}=\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{AWM}}\,\chi_{\mathrm{AWM}}\,A}{N}\frac{t^{2}}{\tau_{\mathrm{AWM}}^{2}}\Big(u_{\theta}-\big(v_{\mathrm{cond}}-J\big[u_{\mathrm{old}},\,t,\,r\big]\big)\Big) (102) and its factor t 2 / τ AWM 2 t^{2}/\tau_{\mathrm{AWM}}^{2} equals one only above the floor. The resulting quantities are x ~ reg = ∅ , x ~ base = sg ⁡ ( x ~ θ ) , x ~ rwd = x ^ , W reg = 0 , W rwd = W = ρ AWM , ω = χ AWM ​ A 2 ​ τ AWM 2 . \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}}=\varnothing,\quad\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}=\operatorname{sg}(\tilde{x}_{\theta}),\quad\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd}}=\hat{x},\quad W_{\mathrm{reg}}=0,\quad W_{\mathrm{rwd}}=W=\rho_{\mathrm{AWM}},\quad\omega=\frac{\chi_{\mathrm{AWM}}\,A}{2\tau_{\mathrm{AWM}}^{2}}. (103) Relative to Equation ( 32 ) with w ⁡ ( t ) = 1 w(t)=1 , the step has an extra factor 1 2 \tfrac{1}{2} , which comes from the Gaussian form of the statistic and is absorbed
by the learning rate. ρ AWM \rho_{\mathrm{AWM}} is not an importance weight, since ( x ~ t , x ^ ) (\tilde{x}_{t},\hat{x}) is not a transition of the rollout; through the gate χ AWM \chi_{\mathrm{AWM}} , it limits one-sided changes of the matching residual
relative to u old u_{\mathrm{old}} . We call this variant MeanFlowAWM . DMDAWM variant. The reported v-aux variant, DMDAWM , drops J J in the current
and old-policy predictions (the J J -free ( t , r ) (t,r) query and x ~ ind | J = 0 {\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{ind}}|_{J=0}} of § 4.3 ) and replaces the reward baseline with the prediction of the auxiliary network: x ~ θ = X [ x ~ t , u θ ( x ~ t , t , r ) , 0 ] , x ~ ind | J = 0 = X [ x ~ t , u old ( x ~ t , t , r ) , 0 ] , x ~ base = x ~ aux , ρ AWM = exp ⁡ [ mean sp ⁡ [ ( x ~ ind | J = 0 − x ^ ) 2 ] − mean sp ⁡ [ ( x ~ θ − x ^ ) 2 ] 2 ​ τ AWM 2 ] , ∂ ℒ ∂ u θ = ρ AWM ​ χ AWM ​ A N ​ t 2 τ AWM 2 ​ ( v ϕ − v cond ) . \begin{gathered}\tilde{x}_{\theta}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r),\,0\big],\qquad{\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{ind}}|_{J=0}}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\mathrm{old}}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r),\,0\big],\qquad\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}=\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{aux}},\\
\rho_{\mathrm{AWM}}=\exp\!\left[\frac{\operatorname{mean}_{\mathrm{sp}}\!\left[({\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{ind}}|_{J=0}}-\hat{x})^{2}\right]-\operatorname{mean}_{\mathrm{sp}}\!\left[(\tilde{x}_{\theta}-\hat{x})^{2}\right]}{2\tau_{\mathrm{AWM}}^{2}}\right],\\
\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial u_{\theta}}=\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{AWM}}\,\chi_{\mathrm{AWM}}\,A}{N}\frac{t^{2}}{\tau_{\mathrm{AWM}}^{2}}\big(v_{\phi}-v_{\mathrm{cond}}\big).\end{gathered} (104) Above the floor the factor t 2 / τ AWM 2 t^{2}/\tau_{\mathrm{AWM}}^{2} equals one; descent moves u θ u_{\theta} along + A ⁡ ( v cond − v ϕ ) +A(v_{\mathrm{cond}}-v_{\phi}) , with v ϕ v_{\phi} as a learned per-sample baseline. If a detached J J were kept in the first two predictions, it would change only the scalar
ratio ρ AWM \rho_{\mathrm{AWM}} and the clipping gate, but not the gradient direction. The
reported method omits it, so it requires no directional-difference evaluation of J J . Decoupled loss. Both variants use Equation ( 90 ) with W reg = 0 W_{\mathrm{reg}}=0 , so only ℒ rwd \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{rwd}} remains.
MeanFlowAWM measures the reward displacement from sg ⁡ ( x ~ θ ) \operatorname{sg}(\tilde{x}_{\theta}) ; DMDAWM measures
it from the auxiliary prediction x ~ aux \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{aux}} .
In code, FewStepAWM implements anchors and weights , and MeanFlowAWM and DMDAWM differ only in their
estimator ( Section 5.4 ). to gradient-guided distribution matching ( Dong et al., 2026 ) .
It implements Example 8 (iii) as a FewStepNFT trainer whose rewarded
points are constructed from the reward gradient instead of sampled; it
therefore uses the normalisers of Equation ( 105 ), and its configuration
with a single ascent step is the ReFL proxy of Example 9 . It modifies
more stages than FewStepNFT. Stage 1 depends on the reward, the rewarded
anchors are constructed points instead of rollout samples, and Stage 4
overrides loss . Stage 2 is unchanged, and r ^ \hat{r} enters
only through the weights. Rollout. FewStepOPSD uses the reward-coupled rollout Equation ( 82 ). It first draws the i.i.d. base samples with Equation ( 81 ). The reward-coupled construction then applies Equation ( 62 ), taking M tgt M_{\mathrm{tgt}} normalised reward-gradient steps in
each direction from the r = 0 r=0 prediction x ~ old , u \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{u}} , with the displacement
transferred to the anchor by Equation ( 70 ), giving x ~ ± \tilde{x}_{\pm} .
The points x ~ ± \tilde{x}_{\pm} are detached, so the computation graph of the reward
ends here and the policy update does not back-propagate through the decoder or
the reward model; the reward must be differentiable. Anchors and weights. (i) Query. Each member is queried at a single level t t , drawn from the
law given in § 6.1 ; hence n it = 1 n_{\mathrm{it}}=1 . As in Example 8 and Example 9 , the query
state is the re-noised endpoint x ~ t = AddNoise ​ ( x ^ , t ) \tilde{x}_{t}=\textsc{AddNoise}(\hat{x},t) , so the anchor is
evaluated at the same state as the current prediction. The drawn r r is stored
and reused at fitting time, so the current prediction and the anchor are
queried at the same ( t , r ) (t,r) . (ii) Anchors. The u-form uses x ~ reg = x ~ base = x ~ old , u \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}}=\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}=\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{u}} and the v-form uses x ~ reg = x ~ base = x ~ ind \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}}=\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}=\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{ind}} , Equation ( 69 ); in both, the rewarded anchors
are x ~ ± \tilde{x}_{\pm} . For the v-form, the gradient steps start from the r = 0 r=0 prediction x ~ old , u \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{u}} , which does not depend on J J , and only the resulting
displacement is added to the anchor x ~ ind \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{ind}} by Equation ( 70 ). (iii) Instantaneous velocity. The v-form uses the shared J J of
Stage 3 (iii) . Because both rewarded points are constructed from the anchor, J J cancels in every difference in the loss, as in Equation ( 71 ) and Equation ( 72 ).
The v-form reported in § 6 drops J J and evaluates no directional
difference ( Equation ( 87 )); in code its estimator is VInd ( cd = False ) . (iv) Weights. With Equation ( 105 ) evaluated at x ~ ± \tilde{x}_{\pm} , W reg = W rwd = W \displaystyle W_{\mathrm{reg}}=W_{\mathrm{rwd}}=W = c adv ​ β 2 ​ ( a + + a − ) , \displaystyle=c_{\mathrm{adv}}\beta^{2}(a_{+}+a_{-}), (107) ω 1 = a + β ⁡ ( a + + a − ) , ω 2 \displaystyle\omega_{1}=\frac{a_{+}}{\beta\,(a_{+}+a_{-})},\quad\omega_{2} = − a − β ⁡ ( a + + a − ) , | ω 1 | + | ω 2 | = 1 β , \displaystyle=-\frac{a_{-}}{\beta\,(a_{+}+a_{-})},\qquad|\omega_{1}|+|\omega_{2}|=\frac{1}{\beta}, so the sum of coefficient magnitudes is fixed at 1 / β 1/\beta and r ^ \hat{r} redistributes it between the two terms. It can still change the common weight W W when γ + ≠ γ − \gamma_{+}\neq\gamma_{-} . The factor c adv c_{\mathrm{adv}} scales the common
weight but not the split between the branches. Decoupled loss. The loss Equation ( 68 ) consists of one
self-regression term and two reward-augmentation terms, which share the
baseline and differ in the rewarded point x ~ rwd , k \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{rwd},k} and the
coefficient ω k \omega_{k} ; the signs of ω k \omega_{k} are fixed by construction and
do not depend on A A . Code 7: FewStepOPSD: reward-coupled construction and loss ⬇ def tr_steps ( x0 , c , direction , M_tgt ): # Eq. The ReFL configuration of Equation ( 68 ), identified in Example 9 , reuses the stages
above, with the changes below. Stage 1 takes a single ascent step, M tgt = 1 M_{\mathrm{tgt}}=1 , without a
descent point. As for FewStepOPSD, the step starts at the r = 0 r=0 prediction
and its displacement is added back by Equation ( 70 ), so ∇ R \nabla R is
evaluated at x ~ old , u \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{u}} as specified by Equation ( 78 ).
Stage 2 is not used, because ReFL has no advantage; ω 1 > 0 \omega_{1}>0 is fixed by
construction and the implementation sets r ^ ≡ 1 \hat{r}\equiv 1 in Equation ( 107 ), giving ω 1 = 1 / β \omega_{1}=1/\beta and W rwd = c adv ​ β 2 / γ + W_{\mathrm{rwd}}=c_{\mathrm{adv}}\beta^{2}/\gamma_{+} , detached and positive as Example 9 allows. The ReFL override keeps the factor c adv c_{\mathrm{adv}} of Equation ( 107 ). Stage 3 keeps the same
anchors. Stage 4 keeps only ℒ rwd , 1 \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{rwd},1} with W reg = 0 W_{\mathrm{reg}}=0 , i.e. the loss of Section 5.6 reduces to its
ascent term ( Section 5.6 ). The resulting gradient is that of Equation ( 79 ), the few-step form of Equation ( 77 ). The cost of one outer iteration is thus C = C rollout + c extra + n it ​ ( c θ + c old + c J + c ϕ + c ref ) C=C_{\mathrm{rollout}}+c_{\mathrm{extra}}+n_{\mathrm{it}}\,(c_{\theta}+c_{\mathrm{old}}+c_{J}+c_{\phi}+c_{\mathrm{ref}}) ,
where C rollout C_{\mathrm{rollout}} is the plain rollout of Stage 1. 6 Experiment Having established a general view of these methods, we use experiments to
answer three questions. Every trainer of
§§ 5.3 – 5.6 implements one of these
methods, minimises the same loss form, and differs mainly in its anchors and
weights. (i) Which of these choices, in particular the reward signal and the
estimator of the instantaneous velocity, determine how effectively and how
efficiently a few-step generator is fine-tuned, and how strongly it exploits the
reward? (ii) Do these
conclusions carry over to a different modality, or do they show limitations in
other scenarios? (iii) Does a generator fine-tuned at a few steps improve when
it is sampled with more steps, without retraining? 6.1 Setup on Image Generation class method reward signal query of u θ u_{\theta} x ~ base \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}} instantaneous field Group-adv. Class: the first five
methods compute scalar rewards for the members of each prompt group and form
per-sample advantages using the per-prompt mean and current
global-batch standard deviation of Equation ( 83 ), so they are
group-advantage-based. The final two obtain
their clean-sample update direction from ∇ R \nabla R and are
reward-gradient-based; FewStepOPSD still uses r ^ \hat{r} only to split the
ascent/descent weights. Reward signal: the advantage as a signed multiplier
( A A ), as a mixing weight ( r ^ \hat{r} , Stage 2), or the reward gradient
( ∇ R \nabla R , Equation ( 62 )). Every
method except FewStepGRPO uses the deterministic sampler of Equation ( 5 ) in Stage 1. Appendix C establishes when dropping J J (last column) is admissible. Generator and sampler. The policy is the average-velocity network u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , r ) u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r) of § 3 , initialised from the pretrained
SD3.5-Medium backbone ( Stability AI, 2024 ; Esser et al., 2024 ) and distilled
with AnyFlow ( Gu et al., 2026 ) , which first trains with the MeanFlow
identity Equation ( 6 ) and then applies on-policy distribution matching Equation ( 10 ) against the frozen teacher.
Reinforcement learning trains a LoRA ( Hu et al., 2022 ) of rank 32 32 (scaling 64 64 ) on the
attention projections, at resolution 512 × 512 512\times 512 . We set M step = 4 M_{\mathrm{step}}=4 steps on the grid of levels { 1 , 0.9 , 0.75 , 0.5 , 0 } \{1,0.9,0.75,0.5,0\} , without classifier-free guidance ( Ho and Salimans, 2021 ) , with the deterministic
sampler of Equation ( 5 ) for every trainer except FewStepGRPO, which
requires the stochastic sampler of Equation ( 94 ). Evaluation always
uses the deterministic sampler on the same grid. Optimisation. In Stage 1 and Stage 2, each outer iteration draws 48 48 prompts with K = 24 K=24 members each, i.e. 1152 1152 rollouts, on 8 8 NVIDIA H20 GPUs. All methods use
AdamW ( Loshchilov and Hutter, 2019 ) with learning rate 3 × 10 − 6 3\times 10^{-6} , weight
decay 10 − 4 10^{-4} and gradient-norm clipping at 1 1 ; an old policy u old u_{\mathrm{old}} given by an EMA of u θ u_{\theta} , refreshed once per outer iteration after the
policy update with the decay schedule of the official DiffusionNFT
implementation ( Section D.1 ); for the NFT/OPSD families, β = 0.1 \beta=0.1 and c adv = 5 c_{\mathrm{adv}}=5 in Equation ( 84 ); and the KL-u penalty against the fixed AnyFlow initial
policy, using Equation ( 92 ) with
weight 10 − 4 10^{-4} . An ablation with the penalty Equation ( 93 ), labelled
KL-v, on MeanFlowNFT is given in Section D.4 .
Stage 3 (i) draws t t and r r as the maximum and the minimum of two
uniforms before the time
shift of the SD3 scheduler, t ↦ 3 ​ t / ( 1 + 2 ​ t ) t\mapsto 3t/(1+2t) (shift 3.0 3.0 , the same
shifted grid as in sampling),
sets half of each batch to r = t r=t and a quarter to r = 0 r=0 , and
uses n ts = 4 n_{\mathrm{ts}}=4 queries per member and outer iteration. The floors
are τ min = 10 − 2 \tau_{\min}=10^{-2} , ε γ = 10 − 5 \varepsilon_{\gamma}=10^{-5} , ε g = 10 − 12 \varepsilon_{g}=10^{-12} and ε std = 10 − 4 \varepsilon_{\mathrm{std}}=10^{-4} , and ε cd = 5 × 10 − 3 \varepsilon_{\mathrm{cd}}=5\times 10^{-3} ( Section D.1 ). Rewards. We use two reward settings to test the methods.
The first is the OCR setting, where the reward is the OCR accuracy of the
text rendered in the image with the OCR prompts. It has no gradient with respect to the clean
sample, so FewStepOPSD and ReFL, which require ∇ R \nabla R , do not apply. In the
mixed-reward setting the reward is the unweighted sum of CLIPScore, HPSv2 and
PickScore on the PickScore prompt set ( Hessel et al., 2021 ; Wu et al., 2023 ; Kirstain et al., 2023 ) . Specific settings. For the group-advantage-based methods, n it = 4 n_{\mathrm{it}}=4 , with n it = M step n_{\mathrm{it}}=M_{\mathrm{step}} for FewStepGRPO
and n it = n ts n_{\mathrm{it}}=n_{\mathrm{ts}} for the others.
FewStepGRPO retains the states of Equation ( 94 ) and uses the base noise scale ς = 0.25 \varsigma=0.25 of ς t \varsigma_{t} in Equation ( 95 ) ( Equation ( 110 )),
with clipping radius ε clip = 4 × 10 − 3 \varepsilon_{\mathrm{clip}}=4\times 10^{-3} (OCR) or 3 × 10 − 3 3\times 10^{-3} (mixed reward) in χ GRPO \chi_{\mathrm{GRPO}} ;
MeanFlowAWM and DMDAWM use radius 10 − 2 10^{-2} (OCR) or 2 × 10 − 3 2\times 10^{-3} (mixed reward) in χ AWM \chi_{\mathrm{AWM}} . For DMDAWM and DMDNFT, the auxiliary
velocity v ϕ v_{\phi} is fitted by Equation ( 88 ) with learning rate 10 − 5 10^{-5} and n fm = 5 n_{\mathrm{fm}}=5 steps per outer iteration. FewStepOPSD uses κ = 0.1 \kappa=0.1 , M tgt = 2 M_{\mathrm{tgt}}=2 and η = 1 \eta=1 in Equation ( 62 ), with ∇ R \nabla R of the full training
reward, drawing t t from a logit-normal law with median 0.278 0.278 (5–95% range [ 0.125 , 0.508 ] [0.125,0.508] ), i.e., the small noise levels of Example 8 , and r r as in Stage 3 (i) . ReFL is Example 9 with M tgt = 1 M_{\mathrm{tgt}}=1 , r ^ ≡ 1 \hat{r}\equiv 1 and ∇ R \nabla R of the full training reward; for a fair
comparison, u old u_{\mathrm{old}} is also the EMA of u θ u_{\theta} .
A summary of the methods is given in Table 5 . Baselines. In the mixed-reward setting we additionally
run the original objectives on the pre-RL few-step model without the
adaptation of § 4.3 , i.e., with the average velocity u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , r ) u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r) , r r drawn as in Stage 3 (i) ( s s for FlowGRPO), substituted for the instantaneous velocity wherever the
original objective requires one. For DiffusionNFT and AWM, it is Equation ( 47 ) with u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , r ) u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r) in place of u θ ​ ( x ~ t , t , 0 ) u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,0) . For FlowGRPO, this substitution is not theoretically solid. We denote these baselines by the names
of the original methods, FlowGRPO, AWM and DiffusionNFT, and compare them with
FewStepGRPO, MeanFlowAWM and MeanFlowNFT, respectively, while keeping the other setting the same. In addition, by Appendix C , the detached
correction J J cancels from its v-form loss, so the u-form in the FewStepOPSD family is the special case
of the v-form in which r r is fixed to 0 0 instead of being drawn at every
query, so we did not compare them. Evaluation. Three sources are used, with the conventions of the
figures. (i) Online, every 50 50 iterations, within training: the aesthetic
score, CLIPScore and OCR accuracy, and in the mixed-reward setting HPSv2 and
PickScore; on DrawBench prompts ( Saharia et al., 2022 ) in the mixed-reward setting, and on the OCR
prompt set for OCR accuracy and for every dimension of the OCR setting. (ii) Offline, at the checkpoints saved every 100 100 iterations, on 256 256 images
with seed 12345 12345 and the same prompts: HPSv3 and ImageReward ( Ma et al., 2025b ; Xu et al., 2023 ) . (iii) Offline,
GenEval2 (Soft-TIFA) ( Kamath et al., 2025 ) , the successor to GenEval ( Ghosh et al., 2023 ) , in its official evaluation setting, following
MeanFlowNFT ( Huang et al., 2026b ) ( Section D.1 ). The LAION aesthetic score ( Schuhmann, 2022 ) , HPSv3,
ImageReward and GenEval2 serve as held-out
evaluators, as do OCR accuracy in the mixed-reward setting and CLIPScore in
the OCR setting. Iteration 0 0 is
the shared AnyFlow Init. checkpoint. 6.2 Setup on Video Generation Generator and sampler. The policy is the few-step generator of
§ 3 on the Wan2.1-T2V-1.3B backbone ( Team Wan et al., 2025 ) ,
distilled on-policy with AnyFlow ( Gu et al., 2026 ) against its frozen teacher as in the image setting. Reinforcement
learning trains a LoRA ( Hu et al., 2022 ) of rank 32 32 (scaling 64 64 ) on the attention
projections, at 480 × 832 480\times 832 and 81 81 frames, i.e. latents of shape 21 × 16 × 60 × 104 21\times 16\times 60\times 104 . We set M step = 4 M_{\mathrm{step}}=4 steps without
classifier-free guidance ( Ho and Salimans, 2021 ) , with the deterministic sampler of Equation ( 5 ) for every method except FewStepGRPO, which requires Equation ( 94 ). Evaluation always uses the deterministic sampler. Optimisation. Each outer iteration draws two batches, each with 2 2 prompts per rank and K = 16 K=16 members, on 32 32 NVIDIA H20 GPUs, i.e. 2048 2048 rollouts per outer iteration. We use AdamW ( Loshchilov and Hutter, 2019 ) with
learning rate 3 × 10 − 6 3\times 10^{-6} and gradient-norm clipping at 1 1 ; u old u_{\mathrm{old}} is an
EMA of u θ u_{\theta} refreshed once per outer iteration; for the NFT/OPSD families, β = 0.1 \beta=0.1 and c adv = 5 c_{\mathrm{adv}}=5 in Equation ( 84 ); the KL-u penalty
against the fixed AnyFlow initial policy uses Equation ( 92 ) with weight 10 − 4 10^{-4} ; and each member is
queried n ts = 4 n_{\mathrm{ts}}=4 times, with half of each batch set to r = t r=t and a
quarter to r = 0 r=0 .
For FewStepOPSD and ReFL, the world size is 48 48 instead, because
computing the gradient with respect to the video latents requires much memory,
while the per-rank batch is unchanged. FewStepOPSD and ReFL therefore use 3072 3072 rollouts per outer iteration, 1.5 1.5 times as many as the 32-GPU runs.
The runs are thus matched in the number of outer iterations but not in the
number of samples, and the quality comparison should be interpreted with this
caveat. GPU-hours remain comparable across runs, whereas s/iter is reported but
is not comparable across runs with different world sizes. Rewards. Using the same four reward components as the
LongCat/MeanFlowNFT video setup ( Meituan LongCat Team et al., 2025 ; Huang et al., 2026b ) , we define our training
reward as an unweighted sum. The HPSv3 scorer ( Ma et al., 2025b ) enters through
HPSv3-general and HPSv3-percentile (per-frame, the latter the mean of the top 30 % 30\% of frames under the caption); VideoAlign’s motion quality (MQ) and text
alignment (TA) are drawn from video-feedback scoring ( Liu et al., 2025b ) . MQ and TA are z z -normalised before
entering the sum; tables report their raw scorer outputs.
Only HPSv3 is differentiable here, and its two reductions account for 96 % 96\% of the aggregate reward under the z z -normalised weights
( Section D.5 ). The VideoAlign reward takes file paths as
input and decodes the videos in its own pipeline, so there is no differentiable
path from the latents to this reward. FewStepOPSD and ReFL therefore use only
the gradients of the HPSv3 terms, while they are evaluated on all four
terms; Section D.5 quantifies this asymmetry, which does not
occur in the image setting. Specific settings. As in the image setting, n it = 4 n_{\mathrm{it}}=4 for
the group-advantage-based methods ( n it = M step n_{\mathrm{it}}=M_{\mathrm{step}} for
FewStepGRPO, n it = n ts n_{\mathrm{it}}=n_{\mathrm{ts}} for the others), and the
auxiliary velocity v ϕ v_{\phi} is fitted by Equation ( 88 ) with learning rate 10 − 5 10^{-5} and n fm = 5 n_{\mathrm{fm}}=5 steps per outer iteration for DMDAWM and
DMDNFT. Because there is one reward setting rather than two, the gate radius ε clip = 10 − 3 \varepsilon_{\mathrm{clip}}=10^{-3} is used for both χ GRPO \chi_{\mathrm{GRPO}} in FewStepGRPO and χ AWM \chi_{\mathrm{AWM}} in
MeanFlowAWM/DMDAWM, and FewStepGRPO uses ς = 0.7 \varsigma=0.7 in Equation ( 95 ).
FewStepOPSD and ReFL require further settings, because the video backbone
imposes three constraints on ∇ R \nabla R that do not occur in the image
setting. First, as stated above, ∇ R \nabla R is computed from
the two HPSv3 reductions instead of the full training reward; the VideoAlign terms still
enter the reward and the advantage but do not contribute to the gradient.
Second, the reward model is cast to fp32 with the sdpa attention
backend; with the released bfloat16 weights, the reward gradient changes direction substantially while the reward value changes by only ∼ 0.003 {\sim}0.003 , and since Equation ( 62 ) normalises ∇ R \nabla R to unit length and thus uses only its direction, this error
directly affects the update. Third, the video is decoded one latent frame at
a time, so ∇ R \nabla R is computed from 21 21 of the 81 81 pixel frames of a
clip, each decoded without its causal prefix; Section D.5 reports the memory measurements that make this necessary. Evaluation. We use two evaluations that measure different
properties. (i) Every 10 10 iterations, we compute the four training rewards on 256 256 held-out prompts with seed 12345 12345 . Since this is the training objective,
it cannot distinguish a quality gain from reward hacking. (ii) At iteration 250 250 , we evaluate VBench ( Huang et al., 2024 ) on 946 946 GPT-augmented
prompts × 5 \times\,5 samples = 4730 =4730 videos per method, with all 16 16 dimensions aggregated by the quality/semantic/overall weights of AnyFlow ( Gu et al., 2026 ) . No method optimises any part of it. Iteration 0 0 is the
shared pre-RL checkpoint trained with AnyFlow. 6.3 Main Results on Image Generation (a) OCR setting OCR accuracy at iteration first it. with at the first saved checkpoint with OCR accuracy > 0.82 >0.82 trainer s/iter 50 100 150 200 OCR ≥ 0.85 \geq 0.85 (h) it. at iteration 400 400 . GenEval2 for the same
runs is shown in Figure 1 . Effectiveness. Table 6 and Figures 4 and 4 support
the following observations. (i) The adaptation of § 4.3 is beneficial in these runs, and the
v-prediction form gives better performance in most of these comparisons
( Figures 6 and 1 ). FewStepGRPO raises the
training reward from 1.450 1.450 to 1.461 1.461 and the held-out HPSv3 from 10.30 10.30 to 11.58 11.58 , whereas FlowGRPO, its counterpart with direct employment of average-velocity, leaves the
training reward at 1.438 1.438 and lowers HPSv3 to 9.62 9.62 . Meanwhile, MeanFlowNFT as
the v-prediction counterpart of DiffusionNFT, which is the direct employment of
average-velocity, reaches a training
reward of 1.509 1.509 against 1.440 1.440 , and is higher on the
held-out HPSv3 ( 13.23 13.23 against 11.79 11.79 ), ImageReward ( 1.385 1.385 against 1.317 1.317 ) and best GenEval2 ( 0.411 0.411 against 0.399 0.399 ), the last within the ∼ 0.03 {\sim}0.03 resolution. (ii) In the OCR setting, DMDAWM, MeanFlowNFT and DMDNFT raise OCR accuracy
from 0.498 0.498 to at least 0.85 0.85 within 100 100 – 150 150 iterations, whereas
MeanFlowAWM and FewStepGRPO reach only 0.802 0.802 and 0.776 0.776 at iteration 200 200 .
Furthermore, it can be observed that within the 200 200 -iteration horizon of Figure 4 , the highest logged OCR
accuracy is reached by MeanFlowNFT, about 0.945 0.945 at iteration 150 150 , and it
is also the first to reach 0.85 0.85 , at iteration 100 100 . DMDAWM starts lower at
iteration 50 50 but rises steeply thereafter and
reaches 0.936 0.936 at iteration 200 200 , where the two curves coincide within the
resolution of the plot ( Table 6 a). Its rapid rise is consistent with a combination of two properties. (i) It has no self-regression
( x ~ reg = ∅ \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}}=\varnothing ), so only reward augmentation contributes to its
decoupled update. (ii) It is DMD-based and models the instantaneous velocity
with the auxiliary network v ϕ v_{\phi} , which is refitted to the current rollouts at
every iteration, so the baseline x ~ base = x ~ aux \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}=\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{aux}} follows the
policy; since this baseline is fixed within an update, reward augmentation uses
the direction A ⁡ ( v cond − v ϕ ) A\,(v_{\mathrm{cond}}-v_{\phi}) instead of one based on the directional-difference
estimate Equation ( 87 ). This fast increase coincides with the large held-out
degradation; at its first checkpoint with OCR accuracy above 0.82 0.82 , HPSv3
drops from 12.86 12.86 to 5.05 5.05 and ImageReward from 1.233 1.233 to 0.092 0.092 . (iii) In the mixed-reward setting, FewStepOPSD and ReFL achieve the two
highest training rewards, with FewStepOPSD highest at the final iteration.

ending at 16.26 16.26 and 16.46 16.46 , slightly below the initialisation. For video, the
reward gradient must be computed for every latent token of a long sequence,
through the decoder and the reward model, which requires several numerical
simplifications in the reward-gradient-based runs. Their VBench point
estimates degrade less than that of DMDNFT in these runs, which does not
establish that they protect against reward exploitation. TA rises above

and ReFL have the second- and third-highest dynamic degree ( 59.44 59.44 and 59.17 59.17 ). Efficiency. The GPU-hours in Table 8 lead to the
following observations. (i) Unlike in the image setting, the DMD-based methods are slower than their
MeanFlow counterparts, in both s/iter ( 821 821 against 734 734 – 737 737 s) and
GPU-hours. With n fm = 5 n_{\mathrm{fm}}=5 , omitting the directional difference saves up to 8 8 forward passes of u old u_{\mathrm{old}} without gradient per member ( n ts = 4 n_{\mathrm{ts}}=4 queries, two each) but adds 5 5 forward and backward passes of v ϕ v_{\phi} per outer
iteration. The fit costs about 144 144 s per iteration and the net s/iter
difference is + 84 +84 – 87 87 s, so the saved J J passes account for about 60 60 s by difference; the fit dominates, plausibly because backward passes through
the larger video model are expensive. (ii) The reward-gradient-based methods lose their cost advantage from the
image setting and are the two most expensive in GPU-hours. FewStepOPSD and ReFL
have a sampling time of up to 674 674 s per iteration (FewStepOPSD), which includes the
reward-gradient computation, against ∼ 291 {\sim}291 s for the other methods, and
an update time of only 112 112 s. The gradient of a video reward with respect to the
latent tokens is expensive, plausibly because the latent sequence is much longer than for
images and because both the reward model and the decoder are larger than their
image counterparts. In addition, MQ and TA are not differentiable
(§ 6.2 ), which further limits these two methods. 6.5 Test-Time Scaling Analysis In deployment, a few-step model should ideally support several operating
points on the quality–latency trade-off, using its training step count for
most requests and more sampling steps when higher quality is required. This
motivates training at a small M step M_{\mathrm{step}} while allowing more solver
steps at inference. If quality improves without retraining, the model supports
test-time scaling and can trade compute for output quality.
Flexible-step generation and inference-time scaling have also been studied for
consistency-type models, improved MeanFlow and scalable generators ( Kim et al., 2024 ; Wang et al., 2024a ; Geng et al., 2026 ; Xie et al., 2025a ) ;
our experiment is restricted to the checkpoints and solver grid below. Setup. We therefore consider the image generation task, and take the checkpoints trained and evaluated
at M step = 4 M_{\mathrm{step}}=4 in § 6.1 and sample each at M step ∈ { 4 , 8 , 16 , 32 } M_{\mathrm{step}}\in\{4,8,16,32\} . We use the checkpoints listed in Table S3 . We
keep the 256 256 DrawBench prompts, seed 12345 12345 , resolution, deterministic sampler Equation ( 5 ), absence of classifier-free guidance, and the time shift 3.0 3.0 of § 6.1 fixed. Increasing M step M_{\mathrm{step}} only refines the same shifted
solver grid; no model is retrained. Thus the experiment isolates a 1 × 1{\times} , 2 × 2{\times} , 4 × 4{\times} , or 8 × 8{\times} inference-step budget.
As references, we also evaluate the AnyFlow initialisation, from which all
seven trainers start, and the pretrained SD3.5-Medium, with the same prompts,
seed, resolution and shifted grid. SD3.5-Medium models the instantaneous
velocity and is not distilled for few-step sampling, so it uses the Euler step Equation ( 4 ) with classifier-free guidance of scale 4.5 4.5 , i.e.,
two network evaluations per step. Figure 7 : Absolute value of each evaluated dimension against M step M_{\mathrm{step}} , one line per fine-tuned model, with the AnyFlow
initialisation (dashed) and the pretrained SD3.5-Medium sampled with classifier-free guidance (dotted) as references. For the fine-tuned
models, the three training-reward summands (CLIPScore, HPSv2 and PickScore)
show little systematic gain from additional steps, while the held-out HPSv3 and
aesthetic score more often decline. Trainer labels are as in Table 5 ;
the checkpoint is given in Table S3 . Figure S1 and Figure S2 in Appendix D show the same results as
percentage changes from the four-step value, grouped by evaluator and by model,
respectively. Main results. Figure 7 shows the per-step trends, and Table S3 in Appendix D reports the percentage changes. We
draw four conclusions. (i) Models at different training stages show different
test-time scaling behaviour. The pretrained SD3.5-Medium models the instantaneous velocity, so four Euler
steps are far too coarse; its scores rise sharply from four to eight steps and
keep improving more slowly up to 32 32 steps (HPSv3 from − 4.49 -4.49 to 9.96 9.96 , Figure 7 ).
Even at 32 32 steps, i.e., 64 64 network evaluations, it remains below every
fine-tuned model at four steps in HPSv3, ImageReward, the aesthetic score,
HPSv2 and PickScore. In contrast, the AnyFlow initialisation, distilled from
SD3.5-Medium for few-step sampling, appears to have already fixed its output patterns at
the step count used in training, and its HPSv3, ImageReward and GenEval2 drop
markedly with more steps; for example, from 4 4 to 32 32 steps, ImageReward
falls from 1.11 1.11 to 1.02 1.02 and GenEval2 from 0.363 0.363 to 0.320 0.320 ( Figure 7 ), beyond the ∼ 0.03 {\sim}0.03 resolution. By comparison, the RL fine-tuned models, which all
start from this initialisation, fluctuate less in these three evaluators. (ii) There is no systematic gain on the training reward. Across the fine-tuned models, the three reward summands remain nearly flat
( Figure 7 and Table S3 ). From 4 4 to 32 32 steps, the held-out
HPSv3 changes by between + 1.0 % +1.0\% and − 6.8 % -6.8\% for six of the seven trainers,
compared with − 17 % -17\% for the AnyFlow initialisation ( Figure 7 ). The stronger degradation
of the initialisation itself suggests that the decline in rewards of the fine-tuned
models is inherited from the initialisation rather than introduced by RL
fine-tuning. Further, these small changes are consistent with the 4 4 -step outputs already having settled into patterns that score well under the
training reward, including sampler-specific reward exploitation.
Doubling to M step = 8 M_{\mathrm{step}}=8 often preserves or slightly improves a
score, but the gain generally saturates or reverses as the grid is refined
further. Figure 8 shows both behaviours; MeanFlowAWM improves at
eight steps and then declines, whereas ReFL declines gradually. (iii) OCR-sensitive prompts can expose a much sharper failure mode. The selected failures occur on malformed or OCR-heavy
prompts. A clear example is shown in Figure 10 , where the DMDNFT and MeanFlowNFT
samples collapse
at M step = 32 M_{\mathrm{step}}=32 to little more than foreground text. HPSv3 falls
from 12.18 12.18 to 0.32 0.32 and from 11.36 11.36 to 0.32 0.32 , respectively, even as
CLIPScore rises from 0.275 0.275 to 0.324 0.324 and from 0.292 0.292 to 0.309 0.309 .
In these selected cases, additional solver steps produce increasingly
text-dominated outputs rather than a visual-quality improvement. (iv) Visible sample-level scaling exists. On inspection, many samples improve with 2 2 – 4 4 times as many
sampling steps, even though the aggregate rewards barely change; on the
selected samples, HPSv3 does rise ( Figure 10 ). At four steps, they
have weak clarity or unstable details, while eight or sixteen steps yield
cleaner and more stable generations; Figure 10 shows two such
cases. The aggregate rewards thus appear less sensitive to these improvements than visual
inspection, and test-time scaling may be useful beyond what the aggregate scores
suggest, although more steps do not improve quality monotonically. Figure 8 : Two selected mild-degradation cases; each panel shows the
six evaluated scores of that image (CLIPScore, HPSv2, PickScore, HPSv3,
aesthetic score and ImageReward). Top, ReFL on the three-cube prompt, where
HPSv3 gradually falls from 15.63 15.63 to 14.78 14.78 . Bottom, MeanFlowAWM on “A
panda making latte art”, where HPSv3 first rises from 11.34 11.34 to 11.59 11.59 at M step = 8 M_{\mathrm{step}}=8 , then falls to 10.77 10.77 at 32 32 as an extra cup appears. Figure 9 : Two selected OCR-sensitive text-dominance cases under test-time scaling: DMDNFT
(top) and MeanFlowNFT (bottom). Within each row, the prompt and seed are fixed
and only M step M_{\mathrm{step}} changes; the values below each image are that
image’s rewards. These are selected qualitative failure cases rather than
population averages. Figure 10 : Selected cases where a modest inference-step increase helps. Top,
FewStepOPSD on the nail-products prompt: the 4 4 -step image is cluttered and
soft, whereas the 8 8 - and 16 16 -step outputs have clearer objects and a more
stable arrangement. 6.6 Practical Guidance The empirical results, each from a single run, suggest the following guidance: (i) Where it is affordable, the reward gradient is our recommended
approach. Driving the update directly by ∇ R \nabla R was the most effective choice
in our mixed-reward image experiments; FewStepOPSD and ReFL achieve the two highest
training rewards in the mixed-reward setting and improve all three summands
(§ 6.3 ). However, the applicability of these methods is
restricted in practice. (i) The gradient can become too expensive when the
reward model and the decoder are large or the latent token sequence is long, as
on video (§ 6.4 ). (ii) The reward must be differentiable
with respect to the clean sample, which excludes OCR accuracy
and VideoAlign’s MQ and TA (§ 6.1 , Section D.5 ). Where both conditions hold, ReFL alone is
already a strong baseline in our runs. (ii) Otherwise, group-advantage-based methods apply. When the reward
gradient is too expensive or unavailable, the reward enters only through the
group advantage, and these methods must convert the average velocity into the
instantaneous one (§ 4.3 ). Among them, the DMD-based methods,
which estimate the instantaneous velocity with the auxiliary network v ϕ v_{\phi} ,
have a lower per-iteration cost in the image setting but a higher one on
video, depending on the relative cost of the saved forward passes for J J and
the added forward and backward passes of v ϕ v_{\phi} (§ 6.3 ,
§ 6.4 ). They need extra optimisation
steps and memory for v ϕ v_{\phi} , and in some of the reported runs their larger reward gains coincide
with larger held-out degradation. (iii) Among the group-advantage-based methods, the DiffusionNFT family
reached high reward values in relatively few iterations. The
FlowGRPO-style replay of FewStepGRPO optimises slowly and has the weakest
observed reward endpoints within the common iteration budget: it barely improves
the mixed reward on images and loses the most reward on video. The DiffusionNFT family
reached high reward values within the common iteration budget in both settings, although the cost
per iteration varies; MeanFlowNFT is expensive in the image setting and DMDNFT
on video. AWM mostly lies between the two in the reported reward outcomes and uses a
PPO-style clip to limit each update. (iv) Test-time scaling of the fine-tuned few-step models saturates quickly. Aggregate scores show no systematic gain; three of the seven HPSv3 curves
peak at the training step count of four and one is tied between four and
eight steps ( Table S3 ). By visual inspection, however, many
individual samples look cleaner with 2 2 – 4 4 times as many steps before the
effect saturates or reverses. Additional steps therefore improve the visual
quality of some samples but do not improve aggregate scores monotonically
(§ 6.5 ), and can degrade sample quality on OCR-sensitive prompts. 7 Conclusion Few-step generators reduce the rollout cost that typically dominates RL post-training of
diffusion models, but they model an average velocity over an interval, whereas
existing RL objectives assume an instantaneous velocity and a many-step
sampler. This paper adapts these objectives to few-step models systematically
in three phases and compares the resulting methods within one codebase. The
transformation to x x -space expresses each objective we consider through a
common policy prediction. The decomposition writes the gradient of each
objective as the sum of a reward-free self-regression and a reward
augmentation, so that a method reduces to a choice of three anchors and three
weights. The adaptation computes these anchors from a few-step model, at the
end of its transport or along its tangent, with the instantaneous velocity
recovered from the network itself through the MeanFlow identity (the
self-induced velocity) or from an auxiliary network. The seven resulting trainers share one training step and are
compared under shared rollout, reward and evaluation code, each in a single
run. Where the reward
is differentiable and its gradient affordable, FewStepOPSD and ReFL, which use
the reward gradient, were the most effective. Otherwise, the DiffusionNFT family
reached high rewards in relatively few iterations, and the DMD-based
variants were faster per iteration on image but slower on video, depending on
the relative cost of forward and backward passes, and in some runs coincided
with larger held-out degradation.
For the fine-tuned models, additional sampling steps gave no systematic
aggregate gain, although many individual samples improve with two to four
times as many steps as in training. Open problems. Several questions remain open. (i) The DMD-based variants estimate the instantaneous velocity with an
auxiliary network, which requires
additional forward and backward passes to fit this network and GPU memory to
hold it. How to reduce this overhead remains open. (ii) Reward-gradient-based methods require a reward that is differentiable
with respect to the clean sample and a gradient that fits in memory. On video,
the motion-quality and text-alignment terms of VideoAlign provide no gradient,
and the per-frame decoding required by GPU memory makes the gradient deviate
strongly from that of a joint decode. Cheaper and more faithful reward
gradients for long latent sequences are needed. (iii) With a scalar group advantage in the mixed-reward setting, whether every reward summand takes effect
depends on the weighting of the summands, on the rescaling by the
global-batch standard deviation in Equation ( 83 ), and on the small
within-group spread of some summands ( Section D.2 ). A general and more
reliable construction of the group advantage remains open. (iv) In our experiments, a few-step model fine-tuned at one step count does not improve
systematically with more steps. Fine-tuning at more step counts may give
test-time scaling but makes the rollouts more expensive. Whether it can be
introduced cheaply during training, or without training, so that a model
fine-tuned at a small step count still improves with more steps at test time,
remains open. References Bergmeister et al. (2026) Andreas Bergmeister, Stefanie Jegelka, Nikolas Nüsken, Carles

B.1 Why there is no auxiliary-velocity variant The auxiliary velocity cannot be used for the current prediction, because
substituting v ϕ v_{\phi} would give ∂ x ~ θ / ∂ θ = 0 \partial\tilde{x}_{\theta}/\partial\theta=0 (§ 4.3 ); the trainers that use v ϕ v_{\phi} therefore use it only for
the baseline, x ~ base = x ~ aux \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}=\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{aux}} ( Equation ( 60 ), Equation ( 104 )). In FewStepGRPO, both anchors that an auxiliary field could
replace are already fixed, x ~ reg = ∅ \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}}=\varnothing and x ~ base = sg ⁡ ( x ~ θ ) = sg ⁡ ( μ θ ) \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}=\operatorname{sg}(\tilde{x}_{\theta})=\operatorname{sg}(\mu_{\theta}) ( Equation ( 97 )), and
§ B.3 shows that this choice reproduces the gradient of the
tempered Gaussian log-score of the action. Apart from the detached J J shared by both means, the old policy enters through μ old \mu_{\mathrm{old}} in ρ ¯ GRPO \bar{\rho}_{\mathrm{GRPO}} . An auxiliary variant would
therefore have to place v ϕ v_{\phi} inside the scoring kernel. (a) v ϕ v_{\phi} as the old-policy field. If μ old \mu_{\mathrm{old}} were replaced by μ ϕ = α t ​ x t − b t ​ v ϕ ​ ( x t , t ) \mu_{\phi}=\alpha_{t}\,x_{t}-b_{t}\,v_{\phi}(x_{t},t) , then at θ = θ old \theta=\theta_{\mathrm{old}} the scoring ratio would compare the distances
of μ ϕ \mu_{\phi} and μ old \mu_{\mathrm{old}} to x r x_{r} . The ratio would then generally differ
from one at zero policy drift, so the PPO clip would respond to the
approximation error of v ϕ v_{\phi} as well as to the change of the policy. (b) v ϕ v_{\phi} as the source of the shared correction. Replacing J J by v ϕ ​ ( x t , t ) − u old ​ ( x t , t , s ) v_{\phi}(x_{t},t)-u_{\mathrm{old}}(x_{t},t,s) in both means of Equation ( 108 ) keeps μ θ − μ old = − b t ​ ( u θ − u old ) \mu_{\theta}-\mu_{\mathrm{old}}=-b_{t}\,(u_{\theta}-u_{\mathrm{old}}) , hence ρ ¯ GRPO = 1 \bar{\rho}_{\mathrm{GRPO}}=1 at θ = θ old \theta=\theta_{\mathrm{old}} , but it requires v ϕ v_{\phi} at the replayed state x t x_{t} . By Equation ( 88 ), v ϕ v_{\phi} is fitted on re-noised states AddNoise ​ ( x ^ , t ) = ( 1 − t ) ​ x ^ + t ​ ϵ \textsc{AddNoise}(\hat{x},t)=(1-t)\hat{x}+t\epsilon , with ϵ \epsilon independent of x ^ \hat{x} , against the conditional velocity v cond = ϵ − x ^ v_{\mathrm{cond}}=\epsilon-\hat{x} . A replayed state
is not of this form, because Equation ( 94 ) adds fresh noise at every
step; hence x t x_{t} and the endpoint x ^ \hat{x} of the same trajectory are not
connected by a straight path with independent noise ϵ \epsilon , and v cond v_{\mathrm{cond}} is not defined at x t x_{t} . Exact transport at every
step would be sufficient for the two state marginals to agree, but the
finite-step map used here is not exact; the replay query can therefore differ
from the distribution on which v ϕ v_{\phi} is fitted. In an earlier run of
construction (b), not among
those of § 6 , which queried v ϕ v_{\phi} at the replayed states, the
evaluated OCR accuracy fell from 0.498 0.498 to 0.016 0.016 within 40 40 iterations
while the loss Equation ( 88 ) decreased from 0.354 0.354 to 0.152 0.152 . This is
consistent with v ϕ v_{\phi} improving on its training distribution while being evaluated on a different
one. Querying v ϕ v_{\phi} at a re-noised state instead removes the mismatch, but
the correction is then evaluated at a state other than the one at which the
kernel is centred, and the scored density is no longer that of the transition
that was taken. The trainers of §§ 5.4 – 5.5 avoid both
problems. Their query state AddNoise ​ ( x ^ , t ) \textsc{AddNoise}(\hat{x},t) follows the training
distribution of v ϕ v_{\phi} (Stage 3 (i) ), and v ϕ v_{\phi} enters only through their
baseline.
FewStepGRPO, whose kernel has to be evaluated at the states the sampler
visited, therefore uses only the shared- J J construction. B.2 The transition kernel Replay trajectory. A state stored on the replay trajectory is not an endpoint-renoise state AddNoise ​ ( x ^ , t ) \textsc{AddNoise}(\hat{x},t) , so v cond v_{\mathrm{cond}} is not its tangent. We instead use ( x t − x ^ ) / t (x_{t}-\hat{x})/t : J ⁡ [ u old , t , s ] = ( t − s ) ​ u old ​ ( x t + ε cd ​ x t − x ^ t , t + ε cd , s ) − u old ​ ( x t − ε cd ​ x t − x ^ t , t − ε cd , s ) 2 ​ ε cd . is τ j \tau_{j} , with τ j = 0 \tau_{j}=0 whenever s j = t j + 1 s_{j}=t_{j+1} , in which case the kernel is
deterministic and has no likelihood ratio. On the four-step grid, with s j s_{j} drawn uniformly from the remaining grid levels, this happens for 52 % 52\% of the
transitions, including every final transition. FewStepGRPO therefore scores the
action under Equation ( 113 ), for three reasons: σ k > 0 \sigma_{\mathrm{k}}>0 on every
transition; the mean is affine in the instantaneous velocity, so that the
equivalence above holds; and the tempered ratio is exact at zero drift by Equation ( 114 ). The approximation lies in the scored transition.

the two kernels. Consequently, ρ ¯ GRPO \bar{\rho}_{\mathrm{GRPO}} is a tempered ratio
of the surrogate scoring kernels and not an exact importance ratio for the

value of ℒ rwd \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{rwd}} is W ​ mean sp ​ [ g 2 ] W\operatorname{mean}_{\mathrm{sp}}\!\left[g^{2}\right] , as for every trainer
(Stage 4). Justification of each factor. • The per-element mean. A Gaussian log-density is a sum over the N N non-batch elements of one latent; taking it as the mean mean sp ⁡ [ ⋅ ] \operatorname{mean}_{\mathrm{sp}}\!\left[\cdot\right] divides the exponent by N N , which is why ρ ¯ GRPO \bar{\rho}_{\mathrm{GRPO}} is the 1 / N 1/N power of the density ratio, or
equivalently a score at temperature N ​ σ k 2 N\sigma_{\mathrm{k}}^{2} . Here N = 65,536 N=65{,}536 for the image latent and N = 2,096,640 N=2{,}096{,}640 for the video latent. Using the mean keeps the ratio within
floating-point range (with the sum, the exponent can overflow for
differences allowed by the clip), follows FlowGRPO, and is consistent with a
clipping radius of order 10 − 3 10^{-3} . • The factor 1 / ( 2 ​ σ k 2 ) 1/(2\sigma_{\mathrm{k}}^{2}) in ω \omega . The reward term of Equation ( 90 ) contributes 2 ​ W ​ ω / N 2W\omega/N times the displacement, so ω \omega must contain the factor 1 / ( 2 ​ σ k 2 ) 1/(2\sigma_{\mathrm{k}}^{2}) of the log-score for Equation ( 115 ) to hold. Without it, each transition would be weighted
by 2 ​ σ k 2 2\sigma_{\mathrm{k}}^{2} , which varies by a factor of 2.7 2.7 across the four transitions of
the grid; a single learning rate cannot compensate for such a
transition-dependent scale. • x ~ base = sg ⁡ ( x ~ θ ) \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}=\operatorname{sg}(\tilde{x}_{\theta}) . The score of the Gaussian at the action, ( x r − μ θ ) / σ k 2 (x_{r}-\mu_{\theta})/\sigma_{\mathrm{k}}^{2} , is measured from the current mean, so the origin of
the reward direction has to follow the policy. A fixed origin μ old \mu_{\mathrm{old}} , i.e. a translation as in DiffusionNFT, coincides with it only at θ = θ old \theta=\theta_{\mathrm{old}} . • x ~ reg = ∅ \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}}=\varnothing . The self-regression role is played by the ratio and
its clip, and by the velocity penalty of Equation ( 92 ); a self-regression term
would add to Equation ( 115 ) a gradient that the policy-gradient
estimator does not contain. • Method-specific c adv c_{\mathrm{adv}} scaling. The implemented NFT
and general OPSD trainers multiply both loss weights W W by c adv c_{\mathrm{adv}} to undo the compression of Equation ( 84 ); the factor is
written explicitly in Equation ( 106 ) and Equation ( 107 ).
ReFL keeps it although it fixes r ^ ≡ 1 \hat{r}\equiv 1 . FewStepGRPO does not use it, since ω \omega already contains
the gated signed advantage χ GRPO ​ A \chi_{\mathrm{GRPO}}A . Appendix C FewStepOPSD This appendix proves that, in the v-form of § 5.6 , the loss

In particular, they are those obtained with J = 0 J=0 . contains J J by Steps 1–5. The penalty of Equation ( 92 ) compares u θ u_{\theta} and u ref u_{\mathrm{ref}} at the same ( x ~ t , t , r ) (\tilde{x}_{t},t,r) and contains no J J either. Corollary C.3 . Dropping J J in Equation ( 116 ) gives x ~ θ = X [ x ~ t , u θ ( x ~ t , t , r ) , 0 ] , x ~ reg = x ~ base = x ~ ind | J = 0 = X [ x ~ t , u old ( x ~ t , t , r ) , 0 ] , x ~ ± = x ~ base + Δ rwd ​ x ~ ± , \begin{gathered}\tilde{x}_{\theta}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\theta}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r),\,0\big],\qquad\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}}=\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}={\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{ind}}|_{J=0}}=X\big[\tilde{x}_{t},\,u_{\mathrm{old}}(\tilde{x}_{t},t,r),\,0\big],\\
\tilde{x}_{\pm}=\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}+\Delta_{\mathrm{rwd}}\tilde{x}_{\pm},\end{gathered} with Δ rwd ​ x ~ ± \Delta_{\mathrm{rwd}}\tilde{x}_{\pm} computed from x ~ old , u \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{u}} . These are the J J -free ( t , r ) (t,r) queries of § 4.3 ; they coincide with the
u-form quantities of § 5.6 (ii) except that the current
prediction and its anchors are queried at ( t , r ) (t,r) instead of ( t , 0 ) (t,0) ; the
reward displacement is still computed from x ~ old , u \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{u}} . At r = 0 r=0 the two
constructions coincide, since then x ~ base = x ~ old , u \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}}=\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{u}} and Δ = 0 \Delta=0 . Freeing r r therefore trains every u θ ​ ( ⋅ , t , r ) u_{\theta}(\cdot,t,r) while the decoder and the reward
gradient are still evaluated at the clean prediction x ~ old , u \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{u}} ; the u-form
trains only u θ ​ ( ⋅ , t , 0 ) u_{\theta}(\cdot,t,0) . C.3 Remarks Remark C.4 (The cancellation as a translation) . Steps 1–4 state that J J shifts the five points x ~ θ \tilde{x}_{\theta} , x ~ reg \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}} , x ~ base \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}} , x ~ + \tilde{x}_{+} , x ~ − \tilde{x}_{-} by the same detached vector − t ​ J -tJ (with x ~ ± \tilde{x}_{\pm} shifted through the offset Δ \Delta of Equation ( 70 )), whereas the loss depends
on them only through x ~ θ − x ~ reg \tilde{x}_{\theta}-\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{reg}} and x ~ ± − x ~ base \tilde{x}_{\pm}-\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}} . The two quantities
of the construction that depend on an absolute position, the points at which ∇ R \nabla R is evaluated and the radius of the trust region, are evaluated at x ~ old , u \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{u}} , which does not depend on J J . Remark C.5 (Where the cancellation fails) . (i) If the gradient steps were started at x ~ ind \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{ind}} itself instead of at x ~ old , u \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{u}} as prescribed by Equation ( 70 ), ∇ R \nabla R would be evaluated at, and the
radius computed from, points displaced by − t ​ J -tJ , and J J would remain in Δ rwd ​ x ~ ± \Delta_{\mathrm{rwd}}\tilde{x}_{\pm} ; this is why Example 8 (iii)
starts the steps at the J J -free prediction. (ii) If the constructed points were left at the end point of the
iteration, x ~ ± [ M tgt ] \tilde{x}^{[M_{\mathrm{tgt}}]}_{\pm} , instead of being shifted back by Δ \Delta ,
the differences x ~ ± − x ~ base \tilde{x}_{\pm}-\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}} would contain Δ \Delta and hence J J .
(iii) The penalty of Equation ( 93 ) contains J J in its target. (iv) An
old policy refreshed between point construction and fitting would make x ~ base \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{base}} at fitting differ from the anchor around which x ~ ± \tilde{x}_{\pm} were placed,
by a term that the cancellation does not remove. Remark C.6 (Why the argument does not extend to the other trainers) . In FewStepNFT, FewStepAWM and FewStepGRPO the rewarded point is a sample (the
rollout sample x ^ \hat{x} or the action x r x_{r} ) and does not contain J J , whereas the
baseline does. Dropping J J changes the reward displacement of FewStepNFT, g = ω ⁡ ( x ^ − x ~ ind ) g=\omega(\hat{x}-\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{ind}}) , by − ω ​ t ​ J -\omega\,tJ (Stage 3 (iii)), and the residual x r − μ θ x_{r}-\mu_{\theta} of FewStepGRPO by − b t ​ J -b_{t}\,J . This shift can be large; for
MeanFlowNFT, the logged mean of | t ​ J | |tJ| is 2.7 2.7 in the mixed-reward setting and 3.6 3.6 in the OCR setting, compared with a root-mean-square entry of the clean
latent of 1.06 1.06 and 1.46 1.46 , respectively. In FewStepOPSD, both ends of every
reward displacement are constructed from the same anchor, and the gradient
steps start from the J J -free prediction x ~ old , u \tilde{x}_{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{u}} ( Equation ( 70 ));
together, these two properties give the cancellation. The same holds for ReFL,
whose single reward square is the first one of Equation ( 68 ). C.4 Cost, and an empirical check J J requires one directional-difference estimate Equation ( 87 ), i.e. two no-grad
forwards of u old u_{\mathrm{old}} . With J J retained, the v-form evaluates it twice per member and outer

dashed lines are the summands of the training reward. D.2 Analysis of no gain in CLIPScore for group-advantage runs In the mixed-reward setting, all five default group-advantage runs end
below the pre-RL CLIPScore. The two runs with the full reward gradient instead
increase CLIPScore at every listed 100 100 -iteration checkpoint, to 0.3217 0.3217 and 0.3172 0.3172 . In contrast, all three CLIP-focused MeanFlowNFT ablations in the lower
block of Table S4 end above the pre-RL value. The training reward weights its three summands equally, but CLIPScore varies
little within a prompt group, where the advantage is computed; at the pre-RL
policy, its within-group standard deviation is only about 0.01 0.01 ( 0.010 0.010 and 0.014 0.014 on the first iteration of the two CLIPScore-only runs below), roughly a
quarter of its global-batch standard deviation and 3.5 3.5 – 5 % 5\% of its value. It
accounts for 30.4 % 30.4\% of the variance of the three summands, against 53.6 % 53.6\% for PickScore and 16.0 % 16.0\% for HPSv2, so an equal weight does not give it an
equal share of the signal. We therefore tested three ways of changing that share, the first two on
MeanFlowNFT ( Table S4 , lower block). (i) Raising the weight of CLIPScore: the weights ( w CLIP , w HPS , w Pick ) = ( 2.4 , 0.3 , 0.3 ) (w_{\mathrm{CLIP}},w_{\mathrm{HPS}},w_{\mathrm{Pick}})=(2.4,0.3,0.3) , i.e. 8 : 1 : 1 8{:}1{:}1 , and ( 3.0 , 0 , 0 ) (3.0,0,0) , CLIPScore alone, the limit of an unbounded
relative weight; both keep K = 24 K=24 . (ii) Enlarging the group: CLIPScore alone with K = 72 K=72 images
per prompt instead of 24 24 . (iii) Removing it from the gradient: ReFL differentiating HPSv2 alone.
The first two increase CLIPScore, and the increase grows with its share; by
iteration 400 400 , it gains 0.0133 0.0133 at 8 : 1 : 1 8{:}1{:}1 , 0.0191 0.0191 with CLIPScore
alone, and 0.0363 0.0363 with K = 72 K=72 , more than either gradient-based trainer
( 0.0286 0.0286 – 0.0331 0.0331 ), although this run draws three times as many images per
prompt. In all three runs, however, the other two summands stop improving, and
HPSv2 and PickScore oscillate within [ 0.2473 , 0.2974 ] [0.2473,\,0.2974] and [ 0.8459 , 0.8691 ] [0.8459,\,0.8691] , the range they occupy at the start of training; under
CLIPScore alone the held-out aesthetic score and OCR accuracy also decrease, by 0.64 0.64 – 0.98 0.98 and 0.18 0.18 – 0.19 0.19 . Conversely, ReFL with the
gradient of HPSv2 alone leaves CLIPScore near its pre-RL value ( 0.2844 0.2844 – 0.2883 0.2883 over
iterations 100 100 – 400 400 ) while raising HPSv2 to 0.3946 0.3946 , the highest of any
reported variant. In these ablations, increasing CLIPScore under a scalar advantage coincides
with the gains of the other summands weakening or vanishing. The scalar is
shared according to the covariance of each summand with the group reward, so
reweighting redistributes the observed signal. The reward gradient instead
contains a direction from every summand; in the reported FewStepOPSD and ReFL
runs, CLIPScore, HPSv2 and PickScore rise together by 0.0286 0.0286 – 0.0331 0.0331 , approximately 0.0705 0.0705 – 0.0764 0.0764 , and approximately 0.0738 0.0738 – 0.0818 0.0818 , respectively.
As noted in the main text, this approach requires a reward that is
differentiable with respect to the clean sample. Iteration 0 0 is the shared pre-RL checkpoint, CLIPScore 0.2886 0.2886 . Conclusion. In the reported group-advantage ablations, raising the
share of CLIPScore or enlarging the group raises CLIPScore while the gains of
the other summands are reduced or vanish. In these runs, the weighting of the
summands therefore affects which rewards improve, not only how fast they
improve. Reward-gradient-based methods provide a direction from every summand,
and all three summands rise together in the reported runs; however, these
methods apply only when the reward is differentiable with respect to the clean
sample, which excludes the OCR setting (§ 6 ). D.3 Why FewStepOPSD resembles ReFL In FewStepOPSD, the signed reward coefficients satisfy | ω 1 | + | ω 2 | = 1 / β |\omega_{1}|+|\omega_{2}|=1/\beta ; r ^ \hat{r} redistributes this magnitude between
the ascent and descent terms and can also change their common weight W W ( Equation ( 107 )). Both terms move the prediction in the direction of
increasing reward, because g 1 = ω 1 ​ Δ rwd ​ x ~ + g_{1}=\omega_{1}\Delta_{\mathrm{rwd}}\tilde{x}_{+} and g 2 = ω 2 ​ Δ rwd ​ x ~ − g_{2}=\omega_{2}\Delta_{\mathrm{rwd}}\tilde{x}_{-} with ω 2 ≤ 0 \omega_{2}\leq 0 , while the two
increments follow + ∇ R +\nabla R and − ∇ R -\nabla R , respectively. The logged mean
magnitudes | ω 1 | = 5.12 |\omega_{1}|=5.12 and | ω 2 | = 4.88 |\omega_{2}|=4.88 differ by 0.24 0.24 , i.e. 2.4 % 2.4\% of their fixed sum 1 / β = 10 1/\beta=10 ; they do
not by themselves determine the imbalance of the two reward-augmentation
vectors. FewStepOPSD is therefore close to the ReFL reward augmentation of
Example 9 , with an added self-regression, a descent term and several
gradient steps; this is consistent with the similar curves in Figure 4 . D.4 The velocity penalty: KL-u against KL-v The velocity penalty, labelled KL in the run names although it is a squared
velocity residual (§ 5.2 ), admits two constructions: the fixed initial
average-velocity reference of Equation ( 92 ), which compares u θ u_{\theta} and u ref u_{\mathrm{ref}} at the same ( x ~ t , t , r ) (\tilde{x}_{t},t,r) (KL-u), and the frozen
instantaneous teacher of Equation ( 93 ), converted through J ⁡ [ u old , t , r ] J[u_{\mathrm{old}},t,r] , which depends on the old policy (KL-v). The variants therefore differ in both
reference construction and parameterisation space, and
the weight is 10 − 4 10^{-4} in both; the main text reports KL-u under the name MeanFlowNFT. In the mixed-reward setting, the two variants are close at iteration 400 400 ;
both reach a training reward of 1.509 1.509 , and the held-out evaluators differ by
about 1 1 – 1.5 % 1.5\% in either direction (HPSv3 13.23 13.23 against 13.36 13.36 ,
ImageReward 1.385 1.385 against 1.365 1.365 ), with neither variant ahead on both.
GenEval2 is 0.407 0.407 against 0.400 0.400 at iteration 400 400 . Its average over
iterations 100 100 – 400 400 is 0.393 0.393 against 0.365 0.365 ; the 0.028 0.028 gap is near the
stated ∼ 0.03 {\sim}0.03 resolution, so we do not interpret it as a clear
difference. In the OCR setting, KL-u reaches OCR accuracy 0.85 0.85 earlier, at iteration 100 100 instead of 150 150 , with 0.785 0.785 , 0.850 0.850 , 0.945 0.945 at iterations 50 50 , 100 100 , 150 150 (the values of Table 6 a) against 0.693 0.693 , 0.834 0.834 , 0.897 0.897 . At matched
OCR accuracy the difference is mixed; at the first saved checkpoint with OCR
accuracy of at least 0.82 0.82 , HPSv3 is 10.64 10.64 against 10.24 10.24 and ImageReward 0.887 0.887 against 0.896 0.896 , each variant ahead on one of the two. At this weight,
the choice of reference therefore has a limited effect in these runs; it changes the iteration
at which the OCR threshold is reached but has no clear effect on the quality at that point, and
both variants have nearly the same cost per iteration ( 435 435 against 436 436 s in the
mixed-reward setting). The main text therefore reports only KL-u. D.5 Partial differentiability of the video reward This restriction does not occur in the image setting and affects only
FewStepOPSD and ReFL. Every method is evaluated on the same four-term reward. A method that
requires ∇ R \nabla R can only use the differentiable terms, which on this
backbone are HPSv3-general ( 39 % 39\% of the sum) and HPSv3-percentile ( 57 % 57\% );
VideoAlign MQ ( 6 % 6\% ) and TA ( − 2 % -2\% ) are not differentiable. The FewStepOPSD
and ReFL runs in Table 8 use the gradients of both HPSv3
reductions, together 96 % 96\% of the aggregate under the logged normalisation,
but no gradient of motion quality or text alignment. On SD3.5, the gradient
covers 100 % 100\% of the training reward, since all three summands are
differentiable image scorers. This restriction is not visible in the reported scalar rewards, since the
reward, the online evaluation and the advantage of FewStepOPSD all use the full
four-term scorer (ReFL has no advantage). The restriction affects the direction
of the gradient and not necessarily the norm of the update. The released bfloat16 HPSv3 scorer changes the reward value by only ∼ 0.003 {\sim}0.003 relative to fp32 but rotates its gradient by 76.2 76.2 degrees on
average. The runs in Table 8 therefore use fp32 with the sdpa attention backend, so this bf16 error does not affect the
reported results. One remaining constraint is that the clip is decoded one
latent frame at a time to limit memory; since the VAE is causal, a frame
decoded alone is treated as the first frame of a clip. Compared with a joint
decode of the same two frames, the gradient deviates by 79.2 79.2 degrees, and the
gradient of the second frame is nearly orthogonal to the joint-decode reference. The FewStepOPSD and ReFL rows of Table 8 therefore use the
gradient of the two HPSv3 terms with per-frame decoding, and not the gradient of
the full reward. Removing the causal-decode constraint requires a joint window
of at least two frames, which needs 126.1 126.1 GB of GPU memory without
activation offloading on a 144 144 GB card with 25.6 25.6 GB already in use, or 189 189 s per iteration with offloading. Across 48 48 GPUs and 250 250 iterations this
amounts to 26.25 26.25 GPU-days (about 0.547 0.547 days of wall-clock time if fully
parallel). This limitation comes from the memory of a single GPU and not
from the method. Experimental support, please view the build logs for errors. Generated by L A T E xml . Instructions for reporting errors We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
